# Moscow

For the oblast in Russia, see [Moscow Oblast](372315.md). "Moskva" redirects here. For other uses, see [Moscow (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_\(disambiguation\)) and [Moskva (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moskva_\(disambiguation\)).

**Moscow**[^cite_note-14] is the [capital](181337.md) and [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_and_towns_in_Russia_by_population) of [Russia](25391.md), situated on the [Moskva River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moskva_\(river\)) in [Central Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Russia). The city has a population estimated at more than 13 million residents within city limits,[^cite_note-2021Census-5] more than 19.1 million residents in the urban area,[^cite_note-Urban-6] and more than 21.5 million residents in the [metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_metropolitan_area).[^cite_note-Megapolis-15] The city covers an area of 2,511 square kilometers (970 sq mi); the urban area covers 5,891 square kilometers (2,275 sq mi);[^cite_note-Urban-6] and the metropolitan area covers more than 26,000 square kilometers (10,000 sq mi).[^cite_note-Megapolis-15] Moscow is among the world's [largest cities](14649921.md): the [most populous city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_cities_by_population_within_city_limits) entirely in Europe,[^cite_note-16] the largest [urban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_urban_areas_in_Europe) and [metropolitan areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_areas_in_Europe) in Europe,[^cite_note-Urban-6][^cite_note-Megapolis-15] and the largest city by land area on the European continent.[^cite_note-Planète_Énergies-17]

First documented in 1147, Moscow became the capital of the [Grand Principality of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Principality_of_Moscow), which led the unification of Russian lands in the 15th century and became the center of a unified state.[^cite_note-18] Following the proclamation of the [Tsardom of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsardom_of_Russia) in 1547, Moscow remained the country's political and economic center for most of its history. During the reign of [Peter the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_the_Great), the Russian capital was moved to the newly founded city of [Saint Petersburg](24320051.md) in 1712, leading to a decline in Moscow's importance throughout the [imperial period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire).[^cite_note-Weeks-19] Following the [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution) and the establishment of the [Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) (Russian SFSR), the capital was moved back to Moscow in 1918.[^cite_note-Weeks-19] The city later became the political center of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) and experienced significant population growth throughout the Soviet period.[^cite_note-Weeks-19] In the aftermath of the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), Moscow remained the capital city of the newly reconstituted Russian Federation and has experienced continued growth.[^cite_note-Weeks-19]

The northernmost and coldest [megacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megacity) in the world, Moscow is governed as a [federal city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_cities_of_Russia),[^cite_note-20] where it serves as the political, economic, cultural, and scientific center of Russia and [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe). Moscow has one of the world's [largest urban economies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_by_GDP).[^cite_note-21] Moscow has the [second-highest number of billionaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_by_number_of_billionaires) of any city (tied with [Hong Kong](13404.md)).[^cite_note-22] The [Moscow International Business Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_International_Business_Center) is one of the largest [financial centers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_centre) in the world and features the majority of [Europe's tallest skyscrapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_Europe). Moscow hosted the [1980 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Summer_Olympics) and co-hosted the [2018 FIFA World Cup](13327177.md).[^cite_note-23]

The city contains several UNESCO [World Heritage Sites](44940.md) and is known for its display of [Russian architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_architecture)—particularly in areas such as [Red Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Square), as well as buildings such as [Saint Basil's Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Basil's_Cathedral) and the [Moscow Kremlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Kremlin), which serves as the seat of power of the [Government of Russia](9787373.md). [Russian companies](268429.md) in various industries are headquartered in Moscow, and the city is served by a comprehensive transit network; this includes [four international airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Airport_\(disambiguation\)#Commercial_passenger_and_cargo_traffic_airports), ten railway terminals, a [tram system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trams_in_Moscow), a [monorail system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Monorail), and the [Moscow Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Metro), which is the busiest metro system in Europe and one of the largest [rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit) systems in the world. More than 40% of the city's territory is covered by greenery, so it is one of the greenest cities in the world.[^cite_note-Planète_Énergies-17][^cite_note-24]

## Etymology

Moscow's name is thought to be derived from that of the Moskva River.[^cite_note-Vasmer-25][^cite_note-Smol-26] Multiple theories have been proposed for the origin of this river's name.

The linguistically best-grounded and most widely accepted theory is that the name comes from the Proto-Balto-Slavic root \**mŭzg*-/*muzg*-, from the [Proto-Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Indo-European) \**meu-* "wet";[^cite_note-Smol2-27][^cite_note-Trubach2-28][^cite_note-29] thus, the name *Moskva* might signify a river at a wetland or marsh.[^cite_note-Vasmer2-30] The name's [cognates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognate) include the following:

- [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language): музга, *muzga* "pool, puddle"

- [Lithuanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_language): *mazgoti* and [Latvian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latvian_language): *mazgāt* "to wash"

- [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit_language): *májjati* "to drown"

- [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language): *mergō* "to dip, immerse"

  [^cite_note-Vasmer2-30]

  [^cite_note-Trubach2-28]

- [Prekmurje Slovene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prekmurje_Slovene): *müzga* "marsh, swamp"

  [^cite_note-31]

In many Slavic countries, *Moskov* is a surname, most commonly in Russia, [Bulgaria](3415.md), [Ukraine](31750.md), and [North Macedonia](23564616.md).[^cite_note-32] In addition, similarly named places exist in [Poland](22936.md), such as [Mozgawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mozgawa).[^cite_note-Vasmer2-30][^cite_note-Smol2-27][^cite_note-Trubach2-28] According to a [Finno-Ugric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finno-Ugric_languages) hypothesis, the [Merya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga_Finns) and [Muroma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muromians) people—who were among the pre-Slavic tribes that inhabited the area—called the river *Mustajoki* ("Black River"), and the name of the river derives from this term.[^cite_note-33] Other theories, having little or no scientific basis, are rejected by linguists.[^cite_note-Vasmer2-30][^cite_note-Smol-26]

The Old Russian form of the city's name has been reconstructed as \*Москы, \**Mosky*;[^cite_note-Vasmer-25][^cite_note-Smol-26] hence the name was one of a few Slavic [*ū*-stem nouns](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Category\:Proto-Slavic%20v-stem%20nouns). As with other nouns of that declension, the name had been undergoing a morphological transformation during an early stage of the language's development; as a result, the first written references in the 12th century were to Московь, *Moskovĭ* ([accusative case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accusative_case)), Москви, *Moskvi* ([locative case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Locative_case)), and Москвe/Москвѣ, *Moskve/Moskvě* ([genitive case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genitive_case)).[^cite_note-Vasmer-25][^cite_note-Smol-26] From the latter forms came the modern Russian form Москва, *Moskva*, which is a result of [morphological generalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morphological_leveling) with numerous Slavic [*ā*-stem nouns](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Category\:Proto-Slavic%20hard%20a-stem%20nouns). The form *Moskovĭ* has left traces in other languages, including the following:

- English: Moscow

- [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language): *Moskau*

  [^cite_note-34]

- [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language): *Moscou*

  [^cite_note-35]

- [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language): *Moscou, Moscovo*

  [^cite_note-36]

- [Spanish](26825.md): *Moscú*

  [^cite_note-37]

Moscow has acquired [epithets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epithet), such as the "[Third Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow,_Third_Rome)".[^cite_note-38] It was one of 12 honorary [Hero Cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hero_City_\(Soviet_Union\)) in the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-39] The [demonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demonym) for a Moscow resident is rendered as *Muscovite* in English.[^cite_note-40]

## History

Main article: [History of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Moscow)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Moscow).

### Prehistory

The site of modern-day Moscow has been inhabited since prehistoric times. Among the earliest archaeological discoveries were relics of the [Lyalovo culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyalovo_culture?action=edit\&redlink=1), which experts assign to the [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) period.[^cite_note-41] These relics confirm that the area's first inhabitants were hunters and gatherers. Around 950 AD, two Slavic tribes—[Vyatichi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vyatichi) and [Krivichi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krivichi)—settled in the area. The Vyatichi may have formed the majority of Moscow's indigenous population.[^cite_note-42]

### Early history (1147–1263)

Further information: [Vladimir-Suzdal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir-Suzdal)

Moscow is first mentioned in chronicles in the year 1147, as part of the principality of [Rostov-Suzdal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rostov-Suzdal), which emerged from the disintegration of [Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kievan_Rus').[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell202345-43] Moscow was referred to as a meeting place for [Yuri Dolgorukiy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuri_Dolgorukiy) and [Sviatoslav Olgovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sviatoslav_Olgovich). At the time, it was a minor town on the western border of the principality.[^cite_note-44] The importance of Moscow increased significantly during the second half of the 12th century, and the town was converted into a fortified *gorod* (stronghold) during the 1150s, when the first walls of the [Kremlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kremlin) were built.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell202346-45] During the [Mongol invasions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Kievan_Rus') of 1237–1238, Moscow was sacked following the destruction of [Ryazan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryazan).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell202346-45]

The first prince of Moscow was [Daniel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_of_Moscow), the youngest son of [Alexander Nevsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Nevsky). In 1263, Daniel was given Moscow as an *[otchina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Votchina)* (hereditary land), where he established a local branch of [Rurikid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rurikids) princes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell202347-46] Two chronicles refer to [Mikhail Khorobrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Khorobrit) as "Mikhail of Moscow" during the mid-13th century, but Daniel is generally considered to be the first prince of Moscow.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell202347-46] On Mikhail's death in 1248—if it is assumed that an [appanage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appanage) principality was created (as a grant to a younger child of a monarch)—Moscow reverted as an [escheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escheat) (where the state takes ownership of "unowned" land) to the grand prince of [Vladimir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir,_Russia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell202347-46] Until 1271, the principality was ruled by the governors of Daniel's uncle [Yaroslav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaroslav_of_Tver), who was given [Tver](372316.md) as an appanage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuchkin2013308-47] Daniel is first mentioned in chronicles in the year 1282, when he participated in a feudal war between his two older brothers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell202347-46]

### Grand Principality (1263–1547)

Main article: [Grand Principality of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Principality_of_Moscow)



The [1382 siege of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Moscow_\(1382\)), miniature from the [Illustrated Chronicle of Ivan the Terrible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illustrated_Chronicle_of_Ivan_the_Terrible)



The [Spasskaya Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spasskaya_Tower), built in 1491



The Sigismundian Plan of Moscow (1610), before the city's destruction in 1612 and changes to streets. The north is to the right.

By 1300, Moscow was one of the leading principalities within [Vladimir-Suzdal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir-Suzdal), alongside [Tver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Tver).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell202348-48] On the right bank of the Moskva River—at a distance of eight kilometers (5 mi) from the Kremlin—Daniel founded the first monastery with the wooden church of St. Daniel-Stylite, which is now the [Danilov Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danilov_Monastery). Following Daniel's death in 1303, the territory of the principality almost tripled in size, encompassing the entire Moskva River along with its [tributaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tributary), which allowed Moscow to become self-sufficient.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell202350–51-49] The principality was also provided with a river network that facilitated trade.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell202355-50]

Daniel's descendants struggled with the princes of Tver over succession to the grand principality.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell202360-51] [Yury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yury_of_Moscow) won recognition as the grand prince from the contemporary Mongol khan in 1318, but Yury lost the title four years later.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell202387-52] [Ivan I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_I_of_Moscow) recovered the grand princely throne from Tver after proving himself to be a loyal servant of the khan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell2023145-53] Ivan collected tribute for the khan of the [Golden Horde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horde) from dependent Russian princes, and he used the funds that he acquired to develop Moscow.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERiasanovskySteinberg201973-54] In addition, the metropolitan of the [Russian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church) found an ally in Ivan and moved his seat from the nominal capital of Vladimir to Moscow.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrummey201440-55][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERiasanovskySteinberg201973-54] The foundation of Moscow's first stone church, the [Dormition Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dormition_Cathedral,_Moscow), was laid in 1326, and the metropolitan chose to be buried there—an act that confirmed Moscow's status as the spiritual center of Russian Orthodoxy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrummey201440-55] Masonry building continued in the following years with the construction of additional stone churches.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmirnova2013273-56] The limestone walls and towers of the Kremlin were built in 1366–1368.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmirnova2013273-56] A distinct [architectural school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_school) emerged in the late 14th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmirnova2013273-56]

The khan of the Golden Horde initially backed Moscow in an effort to halt the eastward expansion of the [Grand Duchy of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Lithuania), but he continued to meddle in Moscow's relations with other Russian princes to prevent it from becoming too strong.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell2023211–212-57] In 1353, the [Black Death spread from northwestern Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death_in_Russia) to Moscow, causing the deaths of ruler [Simeon of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simeon_of_Moscow), his sons, and the metropolitan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell2023217-58] The ruling family of Moscow remained small as a result, and a new vertical pattern was defined: princely succession from father to son.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell2023170–171-59] During the reign of [Dmitry Donskoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Donskoy), the Moscow principality expanded significantly in size.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrummey201449-60] In 1380, Dmitry led a united Russian army to victory over the Mongols in the [Battle of Kulikovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kulikovo), which increased Moscow's prestige and solidified the status of its rulers as military leaders of the nation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrummey201451-61] Following Dmitry's death in 1389, the thrones of Vladimir and Moscow were permanently united.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFennell2023306-62]

During the reign of [Vasily II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasily_II_of_Moscow), a civil war broke out after [Yury of Zvenigorod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yury_of_Zvenigorod) challenged the succession of his nephew in 1425.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrummey201469-63] Moscow switched hands numerous times; Yury's son, [Dmitry Shemyaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Shemyaka), continued to resist until his appanage center of [Galich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galich,_Russia) was captured in 1450.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrummey201475-64] In ecclesiastical matters, Vasily disapproved of the [Council of Florence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Florence), leading him to arrest the metropolitan when he returned in 1441 for having signed it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrummey201472-65] Seven years later, a council of Russian bishops elected their own metropolitan, which amounted to a declaration of [autocephaly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephaly) by the Russian Church.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrummey201472-65] The [fall of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople) in 1453 was viewed by the Russians as divine punishment for its [apostasy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy), and in 1492, Moscow was called an imperial city for the first time by the Russian metropolitan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrummey2014135-66]

During the reign of [Ivan III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_III_of_Russia), nearly all of the Russian states were united with Moscow, and the foundations for a centralized state were laid.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERiasanovskySteinberg201977–79-67] In addition, Ivan's [defeat of the Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Stand_on_the_Ugra_River) in 1480 traditionally marks the end of [Tatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatars) suzerainty.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERiasanovskySteinberg201978-68] Ivan did his utmost to make his capital a worthy successor to Constantinople; he had the Kremlin reconstructed after inviting architects from [Renaissance Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_Italy)—including [Petrus Antonius Solarius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrus_Antonius_Solarius), who designed the new Kremlin wall and its towers; and [Marco Ruffo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marco_Ruffo), who designed the new palace for the prince. The current Kremlin walls were designed by Solarius and completed in 1495. The [Ivan the Great Bell Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_the_Great_Bell_Tower) was built in 1505–1508 and augmented to its current height in 1600. A trading settlement (or *posad*) developed east of the Kremlin, in the area known as *Zaradye*. During the time of Ivan III, the [Red Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Square) appeared, originally called the "Hollow Field".

Ivan's son [Vasily III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasili_III_of_Russia) continued the expansion of the Muscovite state and annexed the remaining Russian territories.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrummey201492RiasanovskySteinberg201979-69] His reign also saw the continued development of the doctrine of Moscow as the "[Third Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow,_third_Rome)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrummey2014136-70] In 1508–1516, the Italian architect [Aleviz Fryazin (Novy)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aloisio_the_New) arranged to construct a moat in front of the eastern wall, which would connect the Moskva and [Neglinnaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neglinnaya_River) Rivers, and be filled with water from the Neglinnaya. Known as the Alevizov moat, it had a length of 541 meters (1,775 feet), a width of 36 meters (118 feet), and a depth of 9.5 to 13 meters (31–43 feet). This moat was lined with limestone; in 1533, it was fenced on both sides with low, four-meter-thick (13-foot) cogged-brick walls.

### Tsardom (1547–1721)

Further information: [Tsardom of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsardom_of_Russia)



[Saint Basil's Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Basil's_Cathedral), built in 1561



The election of [Michael I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_I_of_Russia) as tsar in 1613



The [Kremlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kremlin) during the reign of [Alexis of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexis_of_Russia), 1663

In 1547, [Ivan the Terrible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_the_Terrible) was [crowned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronation_of_the_Russian_monarch) in Moscow as not only the grand prince, but also the first [tsar of all Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsar_of_all_Russia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBushkovitch201148-71] During the 16th and 17th centuries, three circular defenses were built around the town: [Kitay-gorod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kitay-gorod), the White City, and the Earthen City. However, in 1547, fires destroyed much of the town, and in 1571 the [Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Tatars) [captured Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Crimean_Wars), burning everything except the Kremlin.[^cite_note-72] The annals record that only 30,000 of 200,000 inhabitants survived.

The [Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanate_of_Crimea) attacked again in 1591, but they were held back by new defensive walls, built between 1584 and 1591 by a craftsman named [Fyodor Kon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fyodor_Kon). In 1592, an outer earth rampart with 50 towers was erected around the city, including an area on the right bank of the Moscow River. As an outermost line of defense, a chain of strongly fortified monasteries was established beyond the ramparts to the south and east—principally the [Novodevichy Convent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novodevichy_Convent) and the [Donskoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donskoy_Monastery), [Danilov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danilov_Monastery), [Simonov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simonov_Monastery), [Novospasskiy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novospassky_Monastery), and [Andronikov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andronikov_Monastery) monasteries, most of which now house museums. Because of its ramparts, the city became known poetically as *Bielokamennaya*, the "White-Walled". The city limits, as marked by those ramparts, are marked by the [Garden Ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_Ring) in contemporary Moscow. Three square gates existed on the east side of the Kremlin wall, which in the 17th century were known as Konstantino-Eleninsky, Spassky, and Nikolsky (after the icons of Constantine and Helen, the Savior, and St. Nicholas that hung over those gates). The last two gates were directly opposite the Red Square, while the Konstantino-Eleninsky gate was located behind Saint Basil's Cathedral.

The [Russian famine of 1601–1603](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_famine_of_1601%E2%80%931603) killed possibly 100,000 people in Moscow. Between 1610 and 1612, troops of the [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth) occupied Moscow, as its ruler [Sigismund III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_III_Vasa) tried to [take the Russian throne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Russian_War_\(1609%E2%80%931618\)). In 1612, [Nizhny Novgorod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizhny_Novgorod) and other Russian cities, led by prince [Dmitry Pozharsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Pozharsky) and [Kuzma Minin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuzma_Minin), rose against the Polish occupants, [besieged the Kremlin, and expelled them](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_of_Troubles). In 1613, the [Zemsky Sobor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zemsky_Sobor) (lit. 'assembly of the land'; parliament) elected [Michael Romanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_I_of_Russia) as tsar, establishing the [Romanov dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanov_dynasty). The 17th century witnessed several uprisings—such as the liberation of Moscow from Polish–Lithuanian invaders (1612), the [Salt Riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_Riot) (1648), the [Copper Riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper_Riot) (1662), and the [Moscow uprising of 1682](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_uprising_of_1682).

During the first half of the 17th century, Moscow's population doubled from 100,000 to 200,000, and it expanded beyond its ramparts in the latter part of the century. In the middle of the 17th century, 20% of Moscow suburban inhabitants came from the [Grand Duchy of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Lithuania), having been driven from their homeland by Muscovite invaders.[^cite_note-73] By 1682, 692 households were established north of the ramparts—by [Ukrainians](145144.md) and [Belarusians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belarusians) abducted from their hometowns during the [Russo-Polish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Polish_War_\(1654%E2%80%931667\)) of 1654–1667. These new outskirts became known as the *Meshchanskaya [sloboda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sloboda_\(settlement\))* (settlement), after the Ruthenian term *meshchane* meaning "town people". This term acquired a pejorative connotation in 18th-century Russia, and it currently means "petty bourgeois" or "narrow-minded philistine".[^cite_note-74] The entire city of the late 17th century is contained within contemporary Moscow's [Central Administrative Okrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Administrative_Okrug).

Numerous disasters befell the city during this period. [Plague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death) epidemics ravaged Moscow in 1570–1571, 1592, and 1654–1656.[^cite_note-75] The plague killed more than 80% of the population in 1654–1655. Fires burned down much of the wooden city in 1626 and 1648.[^cite_note-76] In 1712, Peter the Great moved his government to the newly built city of Saint Petersburg on the coast of the [Baltic Sea](3335.md).

### Empire (1721–1917)

Main article: [Moscow Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Governorate)

Further information: [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire)

Panorama of Moscow in 1819-1823

A [panoramic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panorama) view of Moscow from the [Spasskaya Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spasskaya_Tower) in 1819–1823

After losing its status as capital, Moscow's population initially decreased—from 200,000 in the 17th century to 130,000 in 1750. However, after 1750, the population grew tenfold over the remaining duration of the Russian Empire, reaching 1.8 million by 1915. The [1770–1772 Russian plague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1770%E2%80%931772_Russian_plague) killed up to 100,000 people in Moscow.[^cite_note-77] By 1700, the construction of cobbled roads had begun. In 1730, permanent street lights were introduced, and by 1867 many streets had a gaslight. In 1883, near the Prechistinskiye Gates, arc lamps were installed. In 1741, Moscow was surrounded by a barricade 40 kilometers (25 mi) long, the Kamer-Kollezhskiy barrier, with 16 gates where customs tolls were collected. The barrier's line is traced in contemporary Moscow by several streets called *val* ("ramparts"). In the early 19th century, the Arch of Konstantino-Eleninsky Gate was paved with bricks; however, the Spassky Gate was the primary front gate of the Kremlin and was used for royal entrances. From this gate, wooden and stone bridges stretched across the moat. Books were sold on this bridge, and stone platforms were built nearby for guns—"raskats". The [Tsar Cannon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsar_Cannon) was located on the [Lobnoye mesto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobnoye_mesto) platform. The road connecting Moscow with Saint Petersburg—the [M10 highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M10_highway_\(Russia\)) in contemporary Russia—was completed in 1746; the road's Moscow end followed the old [Tver](372316.md) road, which had existed since the 16th century. The road became known as *[Peterburskoye Schosse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leningradsky_Prospekt)* after being paved in the 1780s. [Petrovsky Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrovsky_Palace) was built in 1776–1780 by architect [Matvey Kazakov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matvey_Kazakov). Between 1781 and 1804, the Mytischinskiy water pipe (the first in Russia) was built.



Napoleon retreating during the [Fire of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_of_Moscow_\(1812\)), after the failed [French invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_invasion_of_Russia)



[Moskva River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moskva_\(river\)) in the 19th century

When [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_I_of_France) [invaded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon's_invasion_of_Russia) Russia in 1812, Muscovites were evacuated. The [Moscow fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_of_Moscow_\(1812\)) was principally the effect of Russian sabotage. Napoleon's *[Grande Armée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grande_Arm%C3%A9e)* (lit. 'Great Army') was forced to retreat and was nearly annihilated by the devastating Russian winter. In 1813, following the city's destruction during the French occupation, the *Commission for the Construction of the City of Moscow* was established. This commission launched a major program of rebuilding, including a partial replanning of the city center. Among many buildings constructed or reconstructed during this time were the [Grand Kremlin Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Kremlin_Palace) and the [Kremlin Armoury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kremlin_Armoury), the [Moscow University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_University), the [Moscow Manege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Manege) (Riding School), and the [Bolshoi Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshoi_Theatre). [Arbat Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbat_Street) had been in existence since the 15th century or earlier, but this street developed into a prestigious area during the 18th century. The street was destroyed in the fire of 1812 and was rebuilt completely in the early 19th century. [Moscow State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_State_University) was established in 1755. Its main building was reconstructed after the 1812 fire by architect [Domenico Giliardi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domenico_Giliardi). The *[Moskovskiye Vedomosti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moskovskiye_Vedomosti)* newspaper appeared in 1756, originally at weekly intervals, and from 1859 onward as a daily newspaper.

During the 1830s, general [Alexander Bashilov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Bashilov) planned the first regular grid of city streets north of Petrovsky Palace. [Khodynka field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khodynka_Field), south of the highway, was used for military training. Smolensky Rail station (the forerunner of the [Belorussky Rail Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belorussky_Rail_Terminal)) was inaugurated in 1870. [Sokolniki Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sokolniki_Park)—in the 18th century, the home of the tsar's falconers well outside Moscow—became contiguous with the expanding city during the later 19th century and was developed into a municipal park in 1878. The suburban [Savyolovsky Rail Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savyolovsky_Rail_Terminal) was built in 1902. In January 1905, the institution of the City Governor, or [Mayor](101146.md), was officially introduced; Alexander Adrianov became Moscow's first official mayor.

When [Catherine II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_the_Great) assumed power in 1762, observers depicted the city's filth and the smell of sewage as symptoms of the disorderly lifestyles of lower-class Russians recently arrived from the farms. Elites called for improved sanitation, which became part of Catherine's plans for increased control over social life. National political and military successes from 1812 through 1855 calmed her critics and validated efforts to produce a more enlightened and stable society. The poor state of public health was discussed less often. However, in the wake of Russia's failures in the Crimean War in 1855–1856, confidence in the state's ability to maintain order in the slums eroded; therefore, demands for improved public health put this issue back on the public agenda.[^cite_note-78] In 1903, the Moskvoretskaya water supply was completed.

### Soviet period (1917–1991)



City plan of Moscow, 1917

In November 1917, after learning of [the uprising in Petrograd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Revolution) (now called Saint Petersburg), Moscow's [Bolsheviks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsheviks) [started an uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Bolshevik_Uprising). On 2 November \[[O.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates) 15 November] 1917, after heavy fighting, [Soviet power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_power) was established in Moscow.[^cite_note-79] [Vladimir Lenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin), fearing invasion, moved the state's capital back to Moscow on 12 March 1918.[^cite_note-prlib-80] The Kremlin once again became the seat of power, the political center of the new state.

With the [change in societal values imposed by communist ideology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_cultural_heritage_register#Denial_of_heritage_\(1917%E2%80%931941\)), the tradition of preserving cultural heritage was broken. Independent preservation societies, even those that defended only secular landmarks, were disbanded by the end of the 1920s. A new anti-religious campaign, launched in 1929, coincided with the collectivization of peasants; the destruction of churches in cities peaked around 1932. In 1937, letters were written to the [Central Committee of the Communist Party of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Committee_of_the_Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union) to rename Moscow to "Stalindar" or "Stalinodar".[^cite_note-81] Stalin rejected this suggestion.[^cite_note-82]

During World War II, the [State Defense Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Defense_Committee) and the [General Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Staff_of_the_Armed_Forces_of_the_Russian_Federation) of the [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army) were located in Moscow. In 1941, 16 divisions of the national volunteers (more than 160,000 people), 25 battalions, and 4 engineering regiments were formed among Muscovites. Between October 1941 and January 1942, the German [Army Group Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_Group_Centre) was stopped at the outskirts of the city, and then repelled in the [Battle of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Moscow). Many factories were evacuated, together with much of the government, and from 20 October the city was declared to be under siege. Its remaining inhabitants built and manned anti-tank defenses, while the city was bombarded from the air. On 1 May 1944, a [Medal "For the Defence of Moscow"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medal_%22For_the_Defence_of_Moscow%22) was instituted; in 1947, a [Medal "In Commemoration of the 800th Anniversary of Moscow"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medal_%22In_Commemoration_of_the_800th_Anniversary_of_Moscow%22) was instituted. [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_casualties_in_World_War_II) and [Soviet casualties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_casualties_of_the_Soviet_Union) during the battle have been debated, as sources provide different estimates. Total casualties between 30 September 1941 and 7 January 1942 are estimated at 248,000–400,000 for the [Wehrmacht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wehrmacht) and 650,000–1,280,000 for the Red Army.[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85]

| External videos |
| --- |
| video icon [Stalin's Moscow in 1953](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=f9p20dxtTkY) |

During the postwar years, Moscow experienced a [housing crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_crisis), which was solved by the innovation of [high-rise apartments](118767.md). There are more than 11,000 such [standardised and prefabricated apartment blocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Residential_building_series), housing most of Moscow's population—thus, it is by far the country's leading city for high-rise buildings.[^cite_note-hrranking-86] Apartments were built and partly furnished in factories, before being raised and stacked into tall columns. The popular Soviet-era comedy film *[Irony of Fate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irony_of_Fate)* parodies this construction method. The city of [Zelenograd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zelenograd) was built in 1958, 37 kilometers (23 mi) northwest of the city center—along with the [Leningradskoye Shosse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leningradskoye_Shosse) (the M10 highway in contemporary Russia)—and incorporated as one of Moscow's [administrative okrugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Moscow) (divisions). [Moscow State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_State_University) relocated to its campus on [Sparrow Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sparrow_Hills) in 1953.



Stalin's funeral procession, 1953

In 1959, Nikita Khrushchev launched [an anti-religious campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_cultural_heritage_register#Khrushchev's_offensive_\(1959%E2%80%931964\)). Of the 50 churches that were operating in Moscow in 1959, 30 were closed and six demolished. On 8 May 1965, the 20th [anniversary of the Soviet victory](337670.md) in World War II, Moscow was awarded the honorary title of [Hero City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hero_City_\(Soviet_Union\)).

The [Moscow Ring Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Ring_Road) (MKAD) was opened in 1961. It had four lanes running 109 kilometers (68 mi) along city borders. The MKAD marked the administrative boundaries of the city until the 1980s, when outlying suburbs beyond the ring road were incorporated. In 1980, Moscow hosted the [Summer Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Summer_Olympics), which were boycotted by the United States and other Western countries because the Soviet Union invaded [Afghanistan](737.md) in 1979. In 1991, Moscow was the scene of a [coup attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_coup_attempt_of_1991) by conservative communists opposed to the [liberal reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perestroika) of [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev).

### 1991–present

|  | This section needs to be **updated**. The reason given is: **This subsection, titled "1991–present," contains no events after the 2012 territorial expansion, though about 15 years have passed since then. For example, there is no coverage of the 2022– Russo-Ukrainian war and its impact on Moscow. This impact includes drone strikes, effects on resident safety, and economic consequences.**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(September 2026)* |
| --- | --- |

When the USSR was dissolved in 1991, Moscow remained the capital of the [Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Federation). Since then, a market economy has emerged, producing a significant increase in Western-style retailing, services, architecture, and lifestyles. The city continued to grow during the 1990s and 2000s, its population rising from less than nine million to more than ten million. Scholars Mason and Nigmatullina argue that Soviet-era urban-growth controls produced controlled and sustainable metropolitan development, typified by the greenbelt built in 1935. Since then, however, low-density suburban sprawl has increased significantly, as a result of heavy demand for single-family dwellings (rather than multi-family apartments). In 1995–1997, the MKAD ring road was widened from an initial four lanes to ten lanes.

In December 2002, [Bulvar Dmitriya Donskogo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulvar_Dmitriya_Donskogo) became the first Moscow Metro station to open outside the MKAD. The [Third Ring Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Ring_Road_\(Moscow\))—intermediate between the early 19th-century [Garden Ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_Ring) and the Soviet-era outer ring road—was completed in 2004. The greenbelt is becoming more fragmented, and satellite cities are appearing at the fringe. Summer dachas are being converted into year-round residences; with the proliferation of automobiles, the city has heavy traffic congestion.[^cite_note-87] Multiple old churches and other examples of architectural heritage that were demolished during the Stalin era have been restored, such as the [Cathedral of Christ the Saviour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_Christ_the_Saviour). In the 2010s, Moscow's administration launched long-term projects such as the *Moja Ulitsa* (lit. 'My Street') urban redevelopment program[^cite_note-88] and the residence renovation program.[^cite_note-89]

Through territorial expansion on 1 July 2012—southwest into the [Moscow Oblast](372315.md)—the city's area more than doubled, increasing from 1,091 to 2,511 square kilometers (421 to 970 sq mi). As a result, Moscow became the largest city on the European continent by area; Moscow also grew in population by 233,000 people.[^cite_note-Merger2-90][^cite_note-Mos.ru-91] The annexed territory was officially named Новая Москва (lit. 'New Moscow').

## Geography

### Location



Satellite view of Moscow and its nearby suburbs

Moscow is situated on the banks of the Moskva River, which flows for about 500 kilometers (310 mi) through the [East European Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_European_Plain) in Central Russia, not far from the natural border of the forest and the forest-[steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe) zone. A total of [49 bridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_bridges_in_Moscow) span the river and its canals within city limits. Moscow's [elevation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elevation) at the All-Russia Exhibition Center (VVC)—where the leading Moscow weather station is located—is 156 meters (512 ft). [Teplostan Upland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teplostan_Upland) is the city's highest point at 255 meters (837 ft).[^cite_note-92] The width of the city (excluding the [MKAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MKAD)) from west to east is 39.7 kilometers (24.7 mi), and the length from north to south is 51.8 kilometers (32.2 mi).

### Time

Main article: [Moscow Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Time)

Moscow serves as the reference point for the time zone used in most of [European Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Russia), [Belarus](3457.md), and [Crimea](163045.md). These areas operate in [Moscow Standard Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Standard_Time) (MSK, МСК), which is 3 hours ahead of [UTC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UTC) (or [UTC+3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UTC+3)). [Daylight saving time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daylight_saving_time) is no longer observed in Moscow. According to geographical longitude, the average [solar noon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_noon) in Moscow occurs at 12\:30.[^cite_note-93]

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Moscow)



[VDNKh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exhibition_of_Achievements_of_National_Economy) (Exhibition of Achievements of National Economy) after rain

Moscow has a [humid continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate) ([Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification): *Dfb*) with long, cold winters usually lasting from mid-November to the end of March, along with warm summers. More extreme continental climates at the same latitude—such as parts of [Eastern Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Canada) or [Siberia](29639.md)—have much colder winters than Moscow does, suggesting that Moscow receives significant moderation from the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md), even though the city is distant from it. Weather in Moscow can fluctuate widely: in the winter, temperatures range from −25 °C (−13 °F) in the city and −30 °C (−22 °F) in the suburbs to above 5 °C (41 °F); in the summer, temperatures range from 10 to 35 °C (50 to 95 °F).[^cite_note-94]



[Petrovsky Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrovsky_Palace) on [Leningradsky Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leningradsky_Avenue) in winter

During the warm months of June, July, and August, typical high temperatures are 20 to 26 °C (68 to 79 °F). However, during heat waves (which can occur between May and September), daytime high temperatures often exceed 30 °C (86 °F), sometimes for a week or two. In the winter, average temperatures normally fall to approximately −10 °C (14 °F). Nevertheless, most winters have warmer periods with daytime temperatures rising above 0 °C (32 °F), as well as cooler periods with night temperatures falling below −20 °C (−4 °F). These periods usually last a week or two. The growing season in Moscow normally lasts for 156 days, usually around 1 May to 5 October.[^cite_note-95]

The highest temperature ever recorded in Moscow was 38.2 °C (100.8 °F)[^cite_note-96] at the [VVC weather station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VVC_weather_station), as well as 39.0 °C (102.2 °F) in the center of Moscow and at [Domodedovo airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domodedovo_airport); this temperature occurred on 29 July 2010, during the unusual [2010 Northern Hemisphere heat waves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Northern_Hemisphere_heat_waves). Record high and average temperatures were recorded in January, March, April, May, June, July, August, November, and December of 2007–2022.[^cite_note-pogoda_(data)-97] The average July temperature from 1991 to 2020 is 19.7 °C (67.5 °F). The lowest temperature ever recorded was −42.1 °C (−43.8 °F) in January 1940. Snow, which is present for about five months a year, often begins to fall in mid-October; snow cover persists in late November and melts at the end of March.

On average, Moscow receives 1731 hours of sunshine per year, ranging between 8% in December and 52% from May to August.[^cite_note-98] This annual variation is due to convective cloud formation. In the winter, moist air from the Atlantic condenses in the cold continental interior, resulting in overcast conditions. However, this same continental influence results in sunnier summers than oceanic cities of similar latitude such as [Edinburgh](9602.md). Between 2004 and 2010, the average sunshine received varied between 1800 and 2000 hours, with a tendency toward more sunshine in summer months—up to a record 411 hours in July 2014, which represented 79% of potential sunshine. December 2017 was the darkest month in Moscow since records began, with only six minutes of sunlight.[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100]

Temperatures in the center of Moscow are often higher than in the outskirts and nearby suburbs, especially during winter. For example, if the average January temperature in the northeast of Moscow is −6.2 °C (20.8 °F), in the suburbs it is about −8.3 °C (17.1 °F).[^cite_note-101] The temperature difference between the center of Moscow and nearby areas of [Moscow Oblast](372315.md) can sometimes be more than 10 °C (18 °F) on frosty winter nights.

Recent changes in Moscow's regional climate are often cited by climate scientists as evidence of [global warming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_warming), since the city is in the middle latitudes of the Northern Hemisphere;[^cite_note-111] however, by definition, [climate change](5042951.md) is global, not regional. During the summer, very high temperatures are often recorded in the city (in 2001, 2002, 2003, [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Northern_Hemisphere_heat_waves), 2011, and [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_waves_of_2021#June)). Along with the southern part of Central Russia,[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113] after recent years of hot summer seasons, Moscow's climate shows [hot-summer classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate#Koppen_Dfa) trends. Winter has also become significantly milder: for example, the average January temperature in the early 1900s was −12.0 °C (10.4 °F), while it is currently about −7.0 °C (19.4 °F).[^cite_note-114] The end of January–February is often colder, with frosts reaching −30.0 °C (−22.0 °F) a few nights per year (in 2006, 2010, 2011, 2012, and 2013).

As of 2024, the most recent decade was the warmest in the history of meteorological observations of Moscow. Temperature changes in the city are shown in the table below:

## Paleontology

Moscow has internationally significant paleontological monuments in its area.[^cite_note-Malenkina2017-117] One of these is the [Gorodnya River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorodnya_River?action=edit\&redlink=1) with its tributaries, on the banks of which are located outcrops of the [Quaternary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaternary) and older [Cretaceous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretaceous) periods. [Fossils](10958.md) of the [bivalve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bivalve) mollusk *[Inoceramus kleinii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inoceramus)* and tubular passages of burrowing animals—described in 2017 as a new [ichnospecies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ichnospecies), *[Skolithos gorodnensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skolithos)*—were discovered in [Coniacian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coniacian) deposits near the stream bed of the Bolshaya Glinka River.[^cite_note-Malenkina2017-117] The trace fossils [Ichnogenera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ichnogenus) *[Diplocraterion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplocraterion)*, *[Planolites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planolites)*, *Skolithos*, and possibly *[Ophiomorpha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ophiomorpha)* were found in [Albian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albian) deposits. [Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleolithic) flint tools were discovered in the Quaternary deposits of the Bolshaya Glinka stream bed.[^cite_note-Malenkina2017-117]

In 1878, paleontologist [Hermann Trautschold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermann_Trautschold) discovered the left flipper of an [ichthyosaur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ichthyosauria) (an extinct large marine reptile) near the village of Mnevniki, which later became part of Moscow. In 2014, this ichthyosaur was named *[Undorosaurus trautscholdi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undorosaurus)*, after its discoverer. Trautschold determined the age of the sediments from which the specimen was taken to be [Kimmeridgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimmeridgian); however, according to more recent studies, these sediments were formed during the [Tithonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tithonian) age of the [Jurassic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic) period.[^cite_note-Arkhangelsky2014-118]

Other organisms—Albian [foraminifera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foraminifera) and [ammonites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonite)—are also known from Moscow deposits.[^cite_note-Malenkina2017-117]

Fossils of various organisms are displayed in Moscow museums, including the [Orlov Museum of Paleontology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Paleontological_Museum) and the [Vernadsky State Geological Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vernadsky_State_Geological_Museum).

### Vital statistics

The official population of Moscow is based on people holding "[permanent residency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resident_registration_in_Russia)". According to Russia's Federal Migration Service, Moscow has 1.8 million official "guests" who have temporary residency on the basis of visas or other documentation; this results in a legal population of 14.8 million. The number of [illegal immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_immigration_in_Russia)—the vast majority originating from [Central Asia](6742.md)—is estimated to be an additional 1 million people;[^cite_note-134] this results in a total population of about 15.8 million.

**Births and Deaths (2024):[^cite_note-135]**

- Births: 120,215 (9.1 per 1,000 people)

- Deaths: 116,478 (8.9 per 1,000 people)

**Total fertility rate (2024):** 1.46 children per woma&#x6E;**[^cite_note-136]**

**Life expectancy (2021):** Total—74.55 years (male—71.00, female—77.94)[^cite_note-rosstat-137]

Clockwise from left: the [Cathedral of Christ the Saviour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_Christ_the_Saviour), demolished during the Soviet period and reconstructed from 1990–2000; [Cathedral of the Immaculate Conception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_the_Immaculate_Conception_\(Moscow\)); [Moscow Cathedral Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Cathedral_Mosque); and [Moscow Choral Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Choral_Synagogue)

[Christians](5211.md) constitute the majority of the city's population, most of whom follow the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church). The [Patriarch of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_of_Moscow_and_all_Rus') serves as the head of the Church and lives in the [Danilov Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danilov_Monastery). Moscow was called the "city of 40 times 40 churches"—before the Russian Revolution in 1917. Moscow is Russia's capital of [Eastern Orthodox Christianity](10186.md), which has been the [country's traditional religion](3060242.md).

Other religions practiced in Moscow include [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Russia), [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Russia), [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Russia), [Judaism](15624.md), [Yazidism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazidism), and [Rodnovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodnovery) (Slavic Native Faith). The Moscow Mufti Council claimed that [Muslims](19541.md) numbered around 1.5 million of the city's 10.5 million population in 2010.[^cite_note-Spie-140] The city has four [mosques](19894.md).[^cite_note-141]

## Cityscape

See also: [List of tallest buildings in Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_Moscow)

### Architecture



The [State Historical Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Historical_Museum), an example of the [Neo-Russian style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revival_architecture)



The [Cathedral of Christ the Saviour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_Christ_the_Saviour), an example of [Neo-Byzantine architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Byzantine_architecture_in_the_Russian_Empire)

Moscow's architecture is internationally known. Moscow is the site of [Saint Basil's Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Basil's_Cathedral)—with its [onion domes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onion_dome)—as well as the Cathedral of Christ the Saviour and the [Seven Sisters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Sisters_\(Moscow\)) (Stalin-era skyscrapers). The first Kremlin was built in the middle of the 12th century.

Medieval Moscow's design featured concentric walls and intersecting radial thoroughfares. That layout, as well as the city's rivers, helped to shape the city's design in later centuries.

The Kremlin was rebuilt during the 15th century. Its towers and some of its churches were built by Italian architects, lending the city some atmosphere of the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance) period. From the end of the 15th century, Moscow was embellished by masonry structures such as monasteries, palaces, walls, towers, and churches.

The city's appearance had not changed much by the 18th century. Houses were constructed of pine and spruce logs; they had shingled roofs plastered with sod or covered by birch bark. The rebuilding of Moscow during the second half of the 18th century was motivated by continual fires and the needs of the nobility. Much of the old wooden city was replaced by buildings in the classical style.[^cite_note-142]

For much of its history, Moscow's architecture was dominated by Orthodox churches. However, the city's overall appearance changed during Soviet times, especially as a result of [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin)'s large-scale effort to "modernize" Moscow. Stalin's plans for the city included a network of broad avenues and roadways, some of them more than ten lanes wide, which—while significantly simplifying movement through the city—were constructed at the expense of many historical buildings and districts. Among the many losses from Stalin's demolitions were the [Sukharev Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukharev_Tower), an old city landmark, as well as mansions and commercial buildings. The city's new status as the capital of a strongly [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular) nation made religiously significant buildings particularly vulnerable to demolition. Many of the city's churches—in most cases some of Moscow's oldest and most prominent buildings—were destroyed. Notable examples of this destruction include [Kazan Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazan_Cathedral,_Moscow) and the Cathedral of Christ the Saviour. During the 1990s, both cathedrals were rebuilt. Many smaller churches, however, were lost.[^cite_note-143]



[GUM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GUM_\(department_store\)) department store, facing [Red Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Square)



[Ostankino Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostankino_Tower), the tallest freestanding structure in Europe and the [eighth-tallest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_freestanding_structures_in_the_world)

While the later Stalinist period was characterized by the restriction of creativity and architectural innovation, the earlier post-revolutionary years saw many radical buildings created in the city. Particularly notable were the [constructivist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constructivist_architecture) architects associated with [VKHUTEMAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VKHUTEMAS) (the Russian state's art and technical school), responsible for such landmarks as Lenin's Mausoleum. Another prominent architect was [Vladimir Shukhov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Shukhov), famous for Shukhov Tower, one of many [hyperboloid towers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperboloid_structure) that he designed. This one was built between 1919 and 1922 as a transmission tower for a Russian broadcasting company.[^cite_note-stako-144] Shukhov left an enduring legacy in the constructivist architecture of early Soviet Russia. He designed shop galleries, notably the [GUM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GUM_\(department_store\)) department store on [Red Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Square),[^cite_note-stako-144] which was bridged with innovative metal-and-glass vaults.



One of the [Seven Sisters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Sisters_\(Moscow\)), [Hotel Ukraina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radisson_Royal_Hotel,_Moscow), is the [tallest hotel in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_Europe) and one of the [tallest hotels in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_hotels).



[Zhivopisny Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhivopisny_Bridge), the highest cable-stayed bridge in Europe

The most recognizable contributions of the Stalinist period may be the so-called [Seven Sisters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Sisters_\(Moscow\)), seven large skyscrapers distributed around the city at approximately equal distances from the Kremlin. A defining feature of Moscow's skyline, their imposing form was allegedly inspired by the [Manhattan Municipal Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Municipal_Building) in [New York City](645042.md); their style—with intricate exteriors and a large central spire—has been described as [Stalinist Gothic architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalinist_architecture). All seven towers can be seen from most elevated locations in the city. These towers are among the tallest constructions in central Moscow apart from the [Ostankino Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostankino_Tower); when this tower was completed in 1967, it was the highest freestanding land structure in the world. It remains the world's seventy-second tallest, ranked among buildings such as the [Burj Khalifa](794957.md) in Dubai, [Taipei 101](203801.md) in Taiwan, and the [CN Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CN_Tower) in Toronto.[^cite_note-145]

The Soviet goal of providing housing for every family, along with the rapid growth of Moscow's population, led to the construction of large, monotonous housing blocks. Most of these were built after Stalin's era, and their styles are often named after the contemporary leader (e.g., Brezhnev and Khrushchev). These blocks are usually badly maintained.

Although the city retains some five-story apartment buildings constructed before the mid-1960s, more recent apartment buildings are usually at least nine stories tall and have [elevators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elevator). Moscow is estimated to have more than twice as many elevators as New York City and four times as many as [Chicago](6886.md). Moslift, one of the city's major elevator operators, has about 1,500 mechanics on call to respond when residents become trapped.[^cite_note-146]

[Stalinist-era buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalinist_architecture), mostly found in the central part of the city, are large and usually ornamented with [Socialist realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_realism) motifs that imitate [classical](52687.md) themes. However, small churches—almost always [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox)—around the city evoke its past. The Old [Arbat Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbat_Street), a tourist street that was once the center of a bohemian area, retains most of its buildings from before the 20th century. Many buildings found on the inner city's side streets (behind the Stalinist [façades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fa%C3%A7ade) of [Tverskaya Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tverskaya_Street), for example) provide examples of [bourgeois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourgeoisie) architecture typical of Tsarist times. [Ostankino Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostankino_Palace), [Kuskovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuskovo), [Uzkoye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzkoye), and other large estates just outside Moscow originally belonged to nobles during the Tsarist era. Some [convents and monasteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Cloisters_of_Moscow), both inside and outside the city, are open to Muscovites and tourists.



Modern methods of skyscraper construction were first utilized in the city for the [Moscow International Business Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_International_Business_Center) (MIBC).

Attempts are being made to restore many of the city's best-maintained examples of pre-Soviet architecture. These restored structures are distinguished by bright colors and particularly clean façades. There are also a few examples of Soviet [avant-garde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avant-garde) work, such as the house of the architect [Konstantin Melnikov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantin_Melnikov) in the Arbat area. Many of these restorations were criticized for allegedly disrespecting historical authenticity. In addition, [facadism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facadism) is widely practiced.[^cite_note-147] Later examples of Soviet architecture are usually distinguished by large size and semi-[Modernist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernist) styles. An example is the [Novy Arbat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novy_Arbat) project—informally called the "false teeth of Moscow"—and known for widespread disruption of the project's historic area in central Moscow.



Borovitskaya Square—[Monument to Vladimir the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monument_to_Vladimir_the_Great) and [Pashkov House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashkov_House)

Plaques on house exteriors inform pedestrians where a well-known personality once lived. Often, these plaques are dedicated to Soviet celebrities little known outside Russia (and similarly, for decorated generals and revolutionaries, little known inside Russia). In addition, Moscow contains many "museum houses" dedicated to famous Russian writers, composers, and artists.

Moscow's skyline is rapidly modernizing, with several new towers being constructed. In recent years, the city administration has been widely criticized for the widespread destruction of many historical buildings. As much as a third of historic Moscow has been destroyed during the past few years[^cite_note-148] to make space for luxury apartments and hotels.[^cite_note-149] Other historical buildings—including landmarks such as the 1930 Moskva hotel and the 1913 department store Voyentorg—have been razed and rebuilt, with an inevitable loss in historical value. Critics blame the government for not enforcing conservation laws: during the last 12 years, more than 50 buildings with monument status were torn down, several of these dating back to the 17th century.[^cite_note-150] In addition, some critics wonder if the funds used for reconstructing razed buildings could not be used to renovate decaying structures—such as many works of architect Konstantin Melnikov,[^cite_note-151] as well as the [Mayakovskaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayakovskaya_\(Moscow_Metro\)) metro station.

Some organizations, such as the Moscow Architecture Preservation Society[^cite_note-152] and Save Europe's Heritage,[^cite_note-153] are trying to attract international public attention to these problems.[^cite_note-154]

### Parks and landmarks

See also: [List of Moscow tourist attractions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Moscow_tourist_attractions)



[Red Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Square) is a UNESCO [World Heritage Site](44940.md).

Moscow contains 96 parks and 18 gardens, including four [botanical gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botanical_garden). The city contains 450 square kilometers (170 sq mi) of green space in addition to 100 square kilometers (39 sq mi) of forests.[^cite_note-mosru-stats-155] Moscow is green in comparison with other cities of comparable size in Western Europe and North America; this stems partly from a history of green "yards" with trees and grass between residential buildings. Moscow contains on average 27 square meters (290 sq ft) of parks per person, compared with 6 square meters in [Paris](22989.md), 7.5 in [London](17867.md) and 8.6 in New York.[^cite_note-greendress-156]



[Gorky Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorky_Park_\(Moscow\))

[Gorky Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorky_Park_\(Moscow\)) (officially the [Central Park of Culture and Rest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorky_Park_\(Moscow\))), named after writer [Maxim Gorky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxim_Gorky), was founded in 1928. The main area (689,000 square meters or 170 acres)[^cite_note-greendress-156] along the Moskva River contains [estrades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architectural_glossary#Estrade) (raised platforms), children's attractions—including the *Observation Wheel*, as well as ponds with boats and water bicycles—dancing, tennis courts, and other sports facilities. Gorky Park borders the [Neskuchny Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neskuchny_Garden) (408,000 square meters or 101 acres), the oldest park in Moscow and a former imperial residence, created as a result of integrating three estates during the 18th century. The garden features the Green Theater, one of the largest open amphitheaters in Europe, able to contain up to 15 thousand people.[^cite_note-157] Several parks include a section known as a "Park of Culture and Rest", sometimes alongside a wilder area. (Such parks include Izmaylovsky, Fili, and Sokolniki.) Some parks are designated Forest Parks (*lesopark*).



[Dream Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dream_Island_\(amusement_park\)), the largest indoor theme park in Europe

[Izmaylovsky Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Izmaylovsky_Park), created in 1931, is one of the world's largest urban parks, along with [Richmond Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richmond_Park) in London. With an area of 15.34 square kilometers (5.92 sq mi), Izmaylovsky Park is six times larger than [Central Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Park) in New York.[^cite_note-greendress-156]

[Bauman Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauman_Garden_\(Moscow\)), officially founded in 1920 and renamed in 1922 after the [Bolshevik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsheviks) [Nikolay Bauman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolay_Bauman), is one of the oldest parks in Moscow. It lies on the site of the former [Golitsyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Golitsyn) estate and eighteenth-century public garden.[^cite_note-158]



[Novodevichy Convent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novodevichy_Convent) is a UNESCO [World Heritage Site](44940.md).

[Sokolniki Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sokolniki_Park), named after the falcon hunting that previously took place there, is one of the oldest parks in Moscow and has an area of 6 square kilometers (2.3 sq mi). It features a central circle with a large fountain surrounded by alleys of birches, maples, and elms. A labyrinth of green paths lies beyond the park's ponds.

[Losiny Ostrov National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Losiny_Ostrov_National_Park) ("Elk Island" National Park), more than 116 square kilometers (45 sq mi) in area, borders Sokolniki Park and was Russia's first national park. It is wild and known as the "city taiga"—the park contains elk.



The Church of Ascension in [Kolomenskoye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolomenskoye) is a UNESCO [World Heritage Site](44940.md).

The Tsitsin Main [Botanical Garden of Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Botanical_Garden_of_Academy_of_Sciences), founded in 1945, is the largest in Europe.[^cite_note-159] This garden covers 3.61 square kilometers (1.39 sq mi) next to the All-Russia Exhibition Center; it contains a live exhibition of more than 20,000 species of plants from around the world, as well as a lab for scientific research. The garden contains a rosarium (with 20,000 rose bushes), a [dendrarium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dendrarium), and an oak forest (whose average tree age exceeds 100 years). In addition, the garden contains a greenhouse occupying more than 5,000 square meters (53,820 square feet).[^cite_note-greendress-156]

The All-Russian Exhibition Center ([Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language): Всероссийский выставочный центр) was formerly known as the All-Union Agricultural Exhibition (VSKhV) and later the [Exhibition of Achievements of the National Economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VDNKh_\(Russia\)) (VDNKh), though it was officially called a "permanent trade show". This center is a prominent example of Stalinist-era monumental architecture. In this large recreational park, there are scores of pavilions, each representing one of two things: a branch of Soviet industry and science, or a Soviet republic. During the 1990s, the center was—and to some extent still is—misused as a shopping center. (Most pavilions are rented to small businesses.) Nevertheless, the center retains most of its architectural landmarks, including two monumental fountains ([Stone Flower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Stone_Flower_Fountain) and Friendship of Nations) and a 360-degree panoramic cinema. In 2014, the park reverted to the name [Exhibition of Achievements of National Economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exhibition_of_Achievements_of_National_Economy); that same year, a large renovation project was started.[^cite_note-160]

Lilac Park, founded in 1958, contains a permanent sculpture display and a large rosarium.&#x20;

Moscow has long been popular with tourists. Some well-known attractions include one of the city's UNESCO World Heritage Sites—the Moscow Kremlin and Red Square,[^cite_note-161] which were built between the 14th and 17th centuries.[^cite_note-162] The [Church of the Ascension](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolomenskoye#The_White_Column_of_Kolomenskoye) at Kolomenskoye, which dates from 1532, is another UNESCO World Heritage Site and a popular attraction.[^cite_note-163]

Near the [New Tretyakov Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tretyakov_Gallery), there is a sculpture garden, Museon—often called "[the graveyard of fallen monuments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_graveyard_of_fallen_monuments)"—that displays statues from the former Soviet Union that were removed from their places after the USSR's dissolution.

Other Moscow attractions include the [Moscow Zoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Zoo), a zoological garden in two sections (the valleys of two streams) linked by a bridge, containing nearly 1,000 species and more than 6,500 specimens.[^cite_note-moszoo-164] Each year, the zoo attracts more than 1.2 million visitors.[^cite_note-moszoo-164]

Many of Moscow's parks and landscaped gardens are protected natural environments.

| [Zaryadye Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaryadye_Park) | [VDNKh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VDNKh_\(Russia\)) (Exhibition of Achievements of National Economy) | Victory Park on [Poklonnaya Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poklonnaya_Hill) |

## Moscow rings

Moscow's road system is approximately centered around the Kremlin at the city's core. From there, roads generally radiate outward to intersect with a series of circular roads ("rings"):

1. The first and innermost major ring, Bulvarnoye Koltso ([Boulevard Ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulevard_Ring)), was built on the former location of the 16th-century city wall around [Bely Gorod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bely_Gorod) (White Town).

   [^cite_note-golden-ring-165]

   &#x20;The Bulvarnoye Koltso is technically not a ring: it forms an incomplete circle—a [horseshoe-shaped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horseshoe-shaped) arc beginning at the Cathedral of Christ the Saviour and ending at the [Yauza River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yauza_River).

2. The second primary ring, located outside the Boulevard Ring, is the Sadovoye Koltso ([Garden Ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_Ring)). Like the Boulevard Ring, the Garden Ring follows the path of a 16th-century wall that previously encompassed part of Moscow.

   [^cite_note-golden-ring-165]

   

   Moscow as viewed from the [International Space Station](15043.md), 29 January 2014

3. The [Third Ring Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Ring_Road_\(Moscow\)) was completed in 2003 as a high-speed freeway.

4. The Fourth Transport Ring—another freeway—was planned, but it was canceled in 2011. A system of chordal highways will be constructed instead.

In addition to these concentric ring roads, line 5 of the Moscow Metro is circular (hence its name *Koltsevaya Liniya* \[lit. 'ring line']). It is located between the Garden Ring and the Third Transport Ring.

Two overlapping lines of the Moscow Metro form "two hearts"\:t

- **Line 14**: Since 10 September 2016, the [Moscow Central Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Central_Circle) (MCC) renovated railroad (the former *Moskovskaya Okruzhnaya Zheleznaya Doroga*) has been operated as Line 14 of the Moscow Metro. This cone-shaped railroad opened in 1908 (as a freight-only railway from 1934 until the reopening in 2016).

- **Line 11**: Another circular metro line—the Big Circle Line (*[Bolshaya Koltsevaya Liniya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshaya_Koltsevaya_line)*)—opened its first stations in 2018 and the remaining stations in 2023. The [Kakhovskaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakhovskaya_\(Moscow_Metro\))-[Savyolovskaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savyolovskaya_\(Bolshaya_Koltsevaya_line\)) western half of the line was launched in late 2021.

The outermost ring within the city is the [Moscow Ring Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Ring_Road)—often called the MKAD, an acronym for the Russian Московская Кольцевая Автомобильная Дорога—which forms the city's cultural boundary; it was established during the 1950s. It was built at ground level, rather than being elevated, so it forms a barrier to roads that would otherwise pass beneath it. (Before Moscow's 2012 expansion, the MKAD was considered an approximate city boundary.)

Outside Moscow, some roads encompassing the city follow the same circular pattern as within city limits; notable examples are the *Betonka* roads (highways A107 and A108), originally constructed of concrete pads.

To reduce traffic on the MKAD, a new ring road—called *CKAD,* *Centralnaya Koltsevaya Avtomobilnaya Doroga*, Central Ring Roa&#x64;*—*&#x77;as completed outside the MKAD in 2021.
