## Culture

### Museums and galleries

A notable art museum in Moscow is the [Tretyakov Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tretyakov_Gallery), which was founded by [Pavel Tretyakov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavel_Tretyakov), a wealthy patron of the arts who donated a large private collection to the city.[^cite_note-166] The Tretyakov Gallery is divided into two buildings. The Old Tretyakov Gallery—the original gallery in the [Tretyakovskaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tretyakov_Gallery) area on the south bank of the Moskva River—houses traditional Russian works.[^cite_note-trety-167] Art by pre-[Revolutionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Revolution) painters, such as [Ilya Repin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilya_Repin), as well as art by early Russian [icon painters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icon_painting), is also displayed. In addition, the museum contains rare originals by early 15th-century [iconographer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iconography) [Andrei Rublev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrei_Rublev).[^cite_note-trety-167] The New Tretyakov Gallery, created in Soviet times, contains primarily Soviet art, as well as a few contemporary paintings; however, the old and new buildings overlap for early 20th-century art. The new building includes a small reconstruction of [Vladimir Tatlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Tatlin)'s famous *[Monument to the Third International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatlin's_Tower)*, as well as other avant-garde works by artists such as [Kazimir Malevich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazimir_Malevich) and [Wassily Kandinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wassily_Kandinsky). Socialist realism also appears in the new building.



The [Pushkin Museum of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pushkin_Museum)

Another art museum in Moscow is the [Pushkin Museum of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pushkin_Museum), which was founded by (among others) the father of poet [Marina Tsvetaeva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marina_Tsvetaeva). The Pushkin Museum resembles the [British Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Museum) in London in having halls that offer a cross-section of exhibits on world civilizations, with many copies of ancient sculptures. However, the Pushkin Museum also houses paintings from major European eras; works by [Claude Monet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Monet), [Paul Cézanne](24472.md), and [Pablo Picasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Picasso) are included in the museum's collection.

In addition, Moscow contains museums of history, technology, military history, and space exploration. The [State Historical Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Historical_Museum) of Russia ([Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language): Государственный Исторический музей) is located between Red Square and [Manege Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manezhnaya_Square,_Moscow) in Moscow. The museum's exhibitions range from relics of prehistoric tribes in the area of present-day Russia to valuable artworks acquired by the Romanov dynasty. The number of objects in the museum's collection totals several million. The [Polytechnical Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polytechnical_Museum),[^cite_note-168] founded in 1872, is the largest technical museum in Russia, offering an array of historical inventions and technological achievements—including humanoid automata from the 18th century and the earliest Soviet computers. The museum's collection contains more than 160,000 items.[^cite_note-169] The Borodino Panorama[^cite_note-170] museum, located on Kutuzov Avenue, provides an opportunity for visitors to simulate the experience of a battlefield with a 360° [diorama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diorama). This museum is part of the large historical memorial commemorating victory in the Patriotic War of 1812 over Napoleon's army; this memorial also includes a [triumphal arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triumphal_Arch_of_Moscow) erected in 1827. In addition, Moscow contains a military history museum containing statues and military hardware. The [Memorial Museum of Cosmonautics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_Museum_of_Cosmonautics) under the [Monument to the Conquerors of Space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monument_to_the_Conquerors_of_Space) at the end of [Cosmonauts Alley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmonauts_Alley) is the central memorial for Russian space officials.

The [Shchusev State Museum of Architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shchusev_State_Museum_of_Architecture) is the national museum of Russian architecture, named after the architect [Alexey Shchusev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexey_Shchusev). This museum is located near the Kremlin.

The reason given is: **This paragraph states the Moscow will gain its own branch of the Hermitage Museum in 2024. But that date has passed, and the project's current status is unclear. The paragraph needs updating and verification.**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(September 2026)* |

Moscow will gain a branch of the [Hermitage Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermitage_Museum) in 2024. Authorities have agreed on the final project, which will be executed by [Hani Rashid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hani_Rashid), co-founder of the New York-based firm [Asymptote Architecture.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asymptote_Architecture) This firm handled the stock market building in Moscow, the World Business Center Solomon Tower in Busan, and the Strata Tower in Abu Dhabi.[^cite_note-171]





The [Bolshoi Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshoi_Theatre)

### Performing arts and cinema

Moscow is the heart of Russian performing arts, including [ballet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_ballet) and [cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Russia): 68 museums,[^cite_note-172] 103[^cite_note-173] theaters, 132 cinemas, and 24 concert halls. Among Moscow's theaters and ballet studios are the [Bolshoi Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshoi_Theatre), the Malyi Theatre,[^cite_note-174] the [Vakhtangov Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vakhtangov_Theatre), and the [Moscow Art Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Art_Theatre).

The Moscow International Performance Arts Center,[^cite_note-175] opened in 2003—also called the [Moscow International House of Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_International_House_of_Music)—is known for classical music performances. This center contains Russia's largest [organ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organ_\(music\)), which is installed in Svetlanov Hall.

In addition, Moscow contains two large circuses: the [Moscow State Circus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_State_Circus) and the [Moscow Circus on Tsvetnoy Boulevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Circus_on_Tsvetnoy_Boulevard)[^cite_note-176] (also called Nikulin's Circus, after [Yuri Nikulin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuri_Nikulin)).

The [Mosfilm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosfilm) studio was at the heart of many classic films, as it has been responsible for both artistic and mainstream productions.[^cite_note-177] However, despite the ongoing participation and reputation of internationally known Russian filmmakers, previously prolific domestic studios have become less active. The Salut cinema screens rare and historical films, and films from the Museum of Cinema[^cite_note-178] are regularly shown. International film festivals are held in Moscow, including the [Moscow International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_International_Film_Festival), [Stalker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalker_\(film_festival\)) (focusing on human rights), [Artdocfest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artdocfest) (focusing on documentaries), and the [Moscow Jewish Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Jewish_Film_Festival).

## Sports

See also: [Football in Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Moscow)

The [Luzhniki Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzhniki_Stadium) hosted the [1980 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Summer_Olympics) and the [2018 FIFA World Cup Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_FIFA_World_Cup_Final).



[Sparrow Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sparrow_Hills) fan zone during the [2018 FIFA World Cup](13327177.md)



The [SC Olimpiyskiy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_Stadium_\(Moscow\)) stadium was constructed for the [1980 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Summer_Olympics).

More than 500 Olympic champions lived in Moscow as of 2005.[^cite_note-179] The city contains 63 stadiums—in addition to 8 football and 11 light athletics maneges (indoor sports halls); of these, [Luzhniki Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzhniki_Stadium) is the largest in Moscow and the fourth largest in Europe. This stadium hosted the [1998–99 UEFA Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998%E2%80%9399_UEFA_Cup), the [2007–08 UEFA Champions League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007%E2%80%9308_UEFA_Champions_League) finals, the [1980 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Summer_Olympics), and the [2018 FIFA World Cup](13327177.md) (with 7 games in total, including the [final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_FIFA_World_Cup_Final)). Forty other sports complexes are located in the city, including 24 complexes featuring artificial ice. The [Olympic Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_Stadium_\(Moscow_arena\)) in Moscow was the world's first indoor arena for [bandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandy), and it hosted the [Bandy World Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandy_World_Championship) twice.[^cite_note-180] Moscow hosted this competition again in 2010, at the [Krylatskoye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_Palace_Krylatskoye) rink.[^cite_note-181] This rink has also hosted the [World Speed Skating Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Speed_Skating_Championships). In addition, Moscow contains seven [horse racing](74711.md) tracks,[^cite_note-mosru-stats-155] of which the [Central Moscow Hippodrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Moscow_Hippodrome) (founded in 1834) is the largest.[^cite_note-182]



[CSKA Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSKA_Arena) during a game of the [Kontinental Hockey League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kontinental_Hockey_League) (KHL), considered to be the world's second-best ice hockey league

Moscow hosted the [1980 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Summer_Olympics); the yachting events were held in [Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn), [Estonia](28222445.md) (then the [Estonian Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic)). Large sports facilities and the main international airport—Sheremetyevo Terminal 2—were constructed to prepare for the 1980 Summer Olympics. Moscow had previously bid for the [2012 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Summer_Olympics). However, when final voting began on 6 July 2005, Moscow was the first city to be eliminated; those Games were awarded to [London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Summer_Olympics).

[HC CSKA Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HC_CSKA_Moscow) is the most titled ice hockey team in the Soviet Union and Europe. Other large ice hockey clubs from Moscow are [HC Dynamo Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HC_Dynamo_Moscow) (which was the second most titled team in the Soviet Union) and [HC Spartak Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HC_Spartak_Moscow).

The most titled Soviet and Russian club—and one of the most titled [Euroleague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euroleague) clubs—is the basketball club from Moscow, [PBC CSKA Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PBC_CSKA_Moscow). Moscow hosted the [EuroBasket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroBasket) tournament in 1953 and 1965.

Among competing cities, Moscow had the most winners at the [USSR Chess Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USSR_Chess_Championship) and the [Russian Chess Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Chess_Championship).

The most titled volleyball team in the Soviet Union and in Europe (the [CEV Champions League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CEV_Champions_League)) is [VC CSKA Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VC_CSKA_Moscow).

In [football](10568.md) (also known as soccer), [FC Spartak Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FC_Spartak_Moscow) has won the most championship titles in the [Russian Premier League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Premier_League). The team was second only to [FC Dynamo Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FC_Dynamo_Kyiv) in [Soviet times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Top_League). [PFC CSKA Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PFC_CSKA_Moscow) was the first Russian football team to win a [UEFA](32332.md) title, the [UEFA Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004%E2%80%9305_UEFA_Cup) (the contemporary [UEFA Europa League](232175.md)). [FC Lokomotiv Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FC_Lokomotiv_Moscow), [FC Dynamo Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FC_Dynamo_Moscow), and [FC Torpedo Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FC_Torpedo_Moscow) are other professional football teams that are based in Moscow.

- Arenas in Moscow

- Lukoil Arena, home of FC Spartak Moscow

  [Lukoil Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lukoil_Arena), home of [FC Spartak Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FC_Spartak_Moscow)

- VEB Arena, home of PFC CSKA Moscow

  [VEB Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VEB_Arena), home of [PFC CSKA Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PFC_CSKA_Moscow)

- VTB Arena, home of FC Dynamo Moscow and HC Dynamo Moscow

  [VTB Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VTB_Arena), home of [FC Dynamo Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FC_Dynamo_Moscow) and [HC Dynamo Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HC_Dynamo_Moscow)

- RZD Arena, home of FC Lokomotiv Moscow

  [RZD Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RZD_Arena), home of [FC Lokomotiv Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FC_Lokomotiv_Moscow)&#x20;

Other major football, ice hockey, and basketball teams are based in Moscow. Because sports organizations in the Soviet Union were once centralized, two of the best Union-level teams represented defense and law-enforcement agencies: the Armed Forces ([CSKA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSKA_Moscow)) and the Ministry of Internal Affairs ([Dinamo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dynamo_Sports_Club)). Most major cities had army and police sports teams. As a result, Spartak, CSKA, and Dinamo were among the best-funded teams in the USSR.

The [Irina Viner-Usmanova Gymnastics Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irina_Viner-Usmanova_Gymnastics_Palace) is located in the [Luzhniki Olympic Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzhniki_Olympic_Complex). Construction of the Palace started in 2017, and the opening ceremony took place on 18 June 2019. The Palace was financed by billionaire [Alisher Usmanov](13020970.md), husband of the former gymnast and gymnastics coach [Irina Viner-Usmanova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irina_Viner-Usmanova). The total area of the building is 23,500 square meters (253,000 sq ft), which includes three fitness rooms, locker rooms, rooms for referees and coaches, saunas, a canteen, a cafeteria, two ball halls, a medical center, a hall for journalists, and a hotel for athletes.[^cite_note-183]

Because of Moscow's cold climate, [winter sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_sport) are popular. Many of Moscow's parks offer marked trails for skiing and frozen ponds for skating.



The [Luzhniki Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzhniki_Stadium) in Moscow, which hosted games for the [2018 FIFA World Cup](13327177.md)

Moscow hosts the annual [Kremlin Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kremlin_Cup), a tennis tournament on both the [Women's Tennis Association](57768.md) (WTA) and the [Association of Tennis Professionals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Tennis_Professionals) (ATP) tours. This tournament is one of ten Tier-I events on the women's tour, and Russian players participate every year.

The [SC Olimpiyskiy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_Stadium_\(Moscow\)) arena hosted the [Eurovision Song Contest 2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_2009), the first and to date the only [Eurovision Song Contest](9954.md) held in Russia.

[Slava Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slava_Moscow) is a professional rugby club, competing in the national [Professional Rugby League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professional_Rugby_League). Former champions [RC Lokomotiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RC_Lokomotiv_Moscow) joined this league in 2011. The Luzhniki Stadium hosted the [2013 Rugby World Cup Sevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Rugby_World_Cup_Sevens).

In [bandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandy) (a ball game on ice), an internationally successful club is the 22-time [Russian League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Bandy_Super_League) champion [Dynamo Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dynamo_Moscow_Bandy_Club). It has also won the [World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandy_World_Cup) three times and the [European Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Cup_\(bandy\)) six times.

Moscow's [MFK Dinamo Moskva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MFK_Dinamo_Moskva) club is important in European [futsal](188197.md) (football variant), having once won the [Futsal Champions League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Futsal_Champions_League) title.

When Russia was selected to host the [2018 FIFA World Cup](13327177.md), the Luzhniki Stadium's capacity was increased by almost 10,000 seats; in addition, two new stadiums were built: the [Dynamo Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VTB_Arena) and the [Spartak Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lukoil_Arena) (although Dynamo was later excluded from hosting World Cup matches).

## Entertainment

See also: [List of shopping malls in Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_shopping_malls_in_Moscow)



[Arbat Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbat_Street), in the historical center of Moscow

Moscow contains many clubs, restaurants, and bars. [Tverskaya Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tverskaya_Street) is one of the city's busiest shopping streets.

South of Tverskaya Street, in the Kitay-gorod (lit. 'Chinatown') area, the adjoining [Tretyakovsky Proyezd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tretyakovsky_Proyezd) has upscale stores such as [Bulgari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgari), [Tiffany & Co.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiffany_&_Co.), [Armani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armani), [Prada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prada), and [Bentley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bentley).[^cite_note-go-mag-184] Nightlife in Moscow has progressed since Soviet times; the contemporary city has some of the world's largest nightclubs.[^cite_note-185] The most fashionable area is near an old chocolate factory; this area includes bars, nightclubs, galleries, cafés, and restaurants.[^cite_note-186]

[Dream Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dream_Island_\(amusement_park\)) is a Moscow amusement park that opened on 29 February 2020.[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188] It is the largest indoor theme park in Europe. The park covers 300,000 square meters (74 acres). The complex includes a landscaped park, along with a concert hall, a cinema, a hotel, a children's sailing school, restaurants, and shops.

### Moscow authorities



[Moscow City Hall Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Moscow)

According to the [Constitution of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Russia), Moscow is an independent [federal subject](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_subject) of the [Russian Federation](25391.md), a [city of federal importance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_cities_of_Russia).

The [Mayor of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Moscow) is the leading official in the executive, leading the [Government of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Moscow), which is the highest organ of executive power. The [Moscow City Duma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_City_Duma) is the [city duma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_duma) ([city council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_council) or local [parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament)), and local laws must be approved by it. It includes 45 members who are elected for a five-year term on a [single-mandate constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_district) basis.

From 2006 to 2012, direct elections of the mayor were not held because of changes in the [Charter of the city of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_of_the_city_of_Moscow), with the mayor appointed by presidential decree. The first direct elections since the [2003 vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Moscow_mayoral_election) were scheduled to be held after the expiration of the then-current mayor's term in 2015; however, because he voluntarily resigned, these elections took place in [September 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Moscow_mayoral_election).

Local administration is carried out through 11 prefectures—which unify Moscow's districts into administrative districts on a territorial basis—and 125 regional administrations. According to the law "On the organization of local self-government in the city of Moscow", since the beginning of 2003, the executive bodies of local self-government are municipalities; representative bodies are municipal assemblies, whose members are elected in accordance with the Charter of the intracity municipality.

### Federal authorities



[The House of the Government of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_\(Moscow\))

Moscow, as the city designated by the [Constitution of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Russia) as the seat of federal authority, contains the country's federal legislative, executive, and judicial authorities—except for the [Constitutional Court of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Russia), which has been located in [Saint Petersburg](24320051.md) since 2008.

The supreme executive authority—the [Government of the Russian Federation](9787373.md)—is located in the House of the Government of the Russian Federation (the [White House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_\(Moscow\))) on the [Krasnopresnenskaya Embankment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krasnopresnenskaya_Embankment) in the center of Moscow. The [State Duma](1010187.md) is on [Okhotny Ryad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Okhotny_Ryad_\(street\)) (street). The [Federation Council](1268578.md) is in a building on [Bolshaya Dmitrovka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshaya_Dmitrovka_Street) (street). The [Supreme Court of the Russian Federation](5915064.md) is also in Moscow.

The [Moscow Kremlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Kremlin) is the official residence of the President of the Russian Federation. The president's working residence in the Kremlin is located in the [Senate Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kremlin_Senate).

### Safety



A [BMW 5 Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BMW_5_Series) patrol car of the [Moscow Police](32360040.md)

In a ranking of cities by safety that was prepared by *[The Economist](15303116.md)* newspaper in 2021, Moscow was ranked 38th with a score of 62.5 points.[^cite_note-189] The general level of crime is low.[^cite_note-190] More than 170,000 surveillance cameras in Moscow are connected to a facial recognition system. The authorities approved of a two-month experiment with automatic, real-time recognition of faces, gender, and age of people; so these authorities deployed the system around the entire city. A video surveillance network integrates video cameras in 95% of the city's apartment buildings; on the grounds and inside buildings of schools and kindergartens; and in MCC stations, stadiums, public transit stops, bus stations, parks, and underground passages.[^cite_note-191]

Moscow's emergency numbers match those in all other regions of Russia:

- 112—single emergency

- 101—the Fire Service and the [Ministry of Emergency Situations](1568895.md)

- 102—the [police](30975423.md)

- 103—ambulance

- 104—gas emergency

  [^cite_note-192]

Moscow's Emergency Medical Service (EMS) is the second most efficient among the world's megacities, according to the consultancy [PwC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PwC) in a study called "Analysis of EMS Efficiency in Megacities of the World".[^cite_note-193]

## Administrative divisions

Main article: [Administrative divisions of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Moscow)

| Coat of arms |  |
| --- | --- |
| Administrative divisions | 12 |
| Districts | 125 |
| Settlements | 21 |

| [Central Administrative Okrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Administrative_Okrug) [Northern Administrative Okrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Administrative_Okrug) [North-Eastern Administrative Okrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North-Eastern_Administrative_Okrug) [Eastern Administrative Okrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Administrative_Okrug) [South-Eastern Administrative Okrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South-Eastern_Administrative_Okrug) [Southern Administrative Okrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Administrative_Okrug) [South-Western Administrative Okrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South-Western_Administrative_Okrug) [Western Administrative Okrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Administrative_Okrug) [North-Western Administrative Okrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North-Western_Administrative_Okrug) [Zelenogradsky Administrative Okrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zelenogradsky_Administrative_Okrug) [Novomoskovsky Administrative Okrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novomoskovsky_Administrative_Okrug) [Troitsky Administrative Okrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troitsky_Administrative_Okrug) |  |
| --- | --- |



Changes in Moscow's territory from 1922 to 1995

The city of Moscow is led by a single mayor, [Sergey Sobyanin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Sobyanin). The city is divided into 12 administrative *okrugs* and 125 districts.

Moscow's urban planning appeared as early as the 12th century, when the city was founded. The city center grew by amalgamating with suburbs, in keeping with medieval principles of urban development, as strong fortress walls gradually spread along the circular streets of adjacent new settlements. The first circular defense walls established the trajectory for Moscow's rings, laying the groundwork for future city planning.

The following fortifications served as the city's circular defense boundaries at some point in history:&#x20;

- the Kremlin walls

- Zemlyanoy Gorod (Earthwork Town)

- the Kamer-Kollezhsky Rampart

- the Garden Ring

- the small railway ring

The Moscow Ring Road (MKAD) has been Moscow's boundary since 1960. Similarly circular are the main Moscow subway line, the Ring Line, and the so-called Third Automobile Ring (which was completed in 2005). Thus, radial and circular planning continues to define Moscow's development. However, contemporary Moscow has also absorbed a number of areas outside the MKAD—such as Solntsevo, Butovo, and the town of [Zelenograd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zelenograd). Part of [Moscow Oblast](372315.md)'s territory was merged into Moscow on 1 July 2012; as a result, Moscow is no longer fully surrounded by Moscow Oblast, and the city now shares a border with [Kaluga Oblast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaluga_Oblast).[^cite_note-Merger-194] In total, Moscow gained about 1,500 square kilometers (580 sq mi) and 230,000 inhabitants. Moscow's Mayor Sergey Sobyanin praised this expansion as helping Moscow and the neighboring region, a "mega-city" of 20 million people, to develop "harmonically".[^cite_note-Merger2-90]

Each administrative *okrug* and district has its own [coat of arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coat_of_arms) and flag, as well as an individual leader.

In addition to the districts, there are Territorial Units with Special Status. These units usually include areas with small or no permanent populations. Examples include the All-Russia Exhibition Centre, the Botanical Garden, large parks, and industrial zones. In recent years, some territories have been merged with other districts. Moscow has no ethnic-specific areas, such as the [Chinatowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown) in certain [North American](21139.md) and [East Asian](19605700.md) cities. Although districts are not formally designated by income, areas closer to the city center, metro stations, or green zones are considered more prestigious.[^cite_note-195]

Moscow also contains some government bodies of the [Moscow Oblast](372315.md), although the city is not part of the oblast.[^cite_note-MoscowO-196]

## Economy

See also: [Economy of Russia](25705.md)

### Overview

Moscow has one of the [largest municipal economies in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_by_GDP), and the city accounts for more than a fifth of Russia's [gross domestic product](12594.md) (GDP).[^cite_note-198] As of 2021, Moscow's [Gross Regional Product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_Regional_Product) (GRP) reached almost [₽](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruble_sign)24.5 trillion ([US\$](18717338.md)332 billion).[^cite_note-199] The [Gross Metropolitan Product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_metropolitan_product) (GMP) of the Moscow Region was ₽31.3 trillion (around \$425 billion).



[Moscow Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Exchange)

The average gross monthly wage in Moscow is ₽123,688[^cite_note-200] (\$2,000); this wage is around twice the national average of ₽66,572 (\$1,000), and it is one of the highest among the [federal subjects](701438.md) (constituent entities) of Russia.

Moscow is home to the [third-highest number of billionaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_by_number_of_billionaires) among cities internationally,[^cite_note-201] and the highest number among cities in Europe. Moscow is Russia's financial center, as well as headquarters for the country's largest banks and many of its largest companies (e.g., energy company [Rosneft](1313566.md)). Moscow accounts for 17% of Russia's retail sales and 13% of its construction activity.[^cite_note-bofit_42_2010-202][^cite_note-203] Since the [1998 Russian financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Russian_financial_crisis), business sectors in Moscow have shown strong growth. Many business centers and office buildings have been constructed in recent years, but Moscow still has a shortage of office space. As a result, many former industrial and research facilities are being rebuilt for office use. Economic stability has improved overall in recent years; nonetheless, crime and corruption hinder business development.

### Industry

| --- | --- |

Primary [industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industry_\(economics\)) in Moscow include [chemicals](58721.md), [metallurgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallurgy), [food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_industry), [textiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile), [furniture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Furniture), [energy production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_production), [software development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Software_development), and [machinery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine).

A number of industrial organizations are located in Moscow and its surroundings. The [Mil Moscow Helicopter Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mil_Moscow_Helicopter_Plant) manufactures military and civil helicopters. [Khrunichev State Research and Production Space Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khrunichev_State_Research_and_Production_Space_Center) produces space equipment, including modules for the space stations [Mir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mir), [Salyut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salyut), and the [International Space Station](15043.md) (ISS); the center also produces [Proton launch vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proton_\(rocket\)) and military [intercontinental ballistic missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercontinental_ballistic_missile) **(**&#x49;CBMs). In addition, the [Sukhoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi), [Ilyushin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilyushin), [Mikoyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan), [Tupolev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupolev), and [Yakovlev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakovlev) bureaus design aircraft. [Khimki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khimki)—an independent city in [Moscow Oblast](372315.md) that has largely been enclosed by Moscow—contains [NPO Energomash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NPO_Energomash), which produces [rocket engines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_engine) for Russian and American space programs, as well as the [Lavochkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavochkin) design bureau, which built fighter planes during World War II but has switched to [space probes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_probe) since the [Space Race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Race). Automobile plants [ZiL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ZiL) and [AZLK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AZLK), as well as the Voitovich Rail Vehicle plant, are located in Moscow; the [Metrovagonmash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metrovagonmash) metro wagon plant is located just outside city limits. The [Poljot Moscow watch factory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poljot) produces military, professional, and sport watches that are known domestically and internationally.

The Electrozavod factory was Russia's first transformer factory. The Kristall distillery[^cite_note-204] is the oldest in Russia making [vodka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vodka) products, including [Stolichnaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stolichnaya); wines are produced at Moscow plants, including the Moscow Interrepublican Winery.[^cite_note-205] The Moscow Jewelry Factory[^cite_note-206] and the Jewellerprom[^cite_note-207] produce jewelry.

Other industries are located just outside Moscow, and some microelectronic industries are located in [Zelenograd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zelenograd), including the company [Ruselectronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruselectronics).

Gazprom, the world's largest extractor of natural gas and the largest [Russian company](25705.md), is headquartered in Moscow, along with other oil, gas, and electricity companies.

Moscow contains the headquarters of many [telecommunications](33094374.md) and [technology](44211754.md) companies, including [1C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1C_Company), [ABBYY](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABBYY), [Beeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beeline_\(brand\)), [Kaspersky Lab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaspersky_Lab), [Mail.Ru Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mail.Ru), [MegaFon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MegaFon), [MTS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MTS_\(network_provider\)), [Rambler\&Co](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rambler_\(portal\)), [Rostelecom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rostelecom), [Yandex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yandex), and [Yota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yota).

Some industry is being transferred out of Moscow to improve the city's ecological condition.

[Tretyakovsky Proyezd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tretyakovsky_Proyezd)



[Nikolskaya Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolskaya_Street)



A [microdistrict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microdistrict) in [Mitino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitino_District) built during the 1990s

During the [Soviet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) period, apartments were lent to residents by the government according to a norm of square meters per person. Certain groups—including [People's Artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Artist_of_the_USSR), heroes, and prominent scientists—received bonuses proportional to their honors. [Private ownership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_ownership) of apartments was limited until the 1990s, when residents were permitted to acquire rights to their inhabited properties. Since the Soviet era, apartment owners have needed to pay a service charge for their residences, a fixed amount based on residents per living area.

|  | This section needs to be **updated**. The reason given is: **The paragraph on real estate prices presents specific dollar figures as current, but the cited sources range from 2006 to 2021. These citations are between 5 and 20 years out of date. The figures may also be internally inconsistent, reflecting prices from different years.**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(September 2026)* |
| --- | --- |

The price of real estate in Moscow continues to increase. The expected price averages \$4,000 per square meter (11 sq ft) near the perimeter of the city[^cite_note-208] or \$6,500–8,000 per square meter in a prestigious district. The price sometimes exceeds \$40,000 per square meter for a flat.[^cite_note-209][^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211] Renting costs about \$1,200 per month for a one-bedroom apartment and about \$1,000 per month for a studio in the city center.

A typical one-bedroom apartment in Moscow is about 30 square meters (320 square feet) in area; a typical two-bedroom apartment is 45 square meters (480 square feet); and a typical three-bedroom apartment is 70 square meters (750 square feet). Many residents cannot move out of their apartments, especially if a family lives in a two-room apartment originally granted by the state during the Soviet era. Some city residents have attempted to cope with the cost of living by renting out their apartments while staying in [dachas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dacha) (country houses) outside the city.

In 2006, Mercer Human Resources Consulting named Moscow the world's [most expensive city for expatriate employees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most_expensive_cities_for_expatriate_employees), ahead of recurring winner Tokyo, because of the stable [Russian ruble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_ruble) as well as increasing housing prices.[^cite_note-212] Moscow also ranked first in the 2007 edition and 2008 edition of the survey.[^cite_note-213] However, in 2009, Tokyo overtook Moscow, which came in third after Osaka.[^cite_note-mercer.com-214]

In 2014, according to *[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)* magazine, Moscow was ranked the world's ninth most expensive city. This magazine ranked Moscow the second most expensive city the previous year.[^cite_note-215]

In 2019, the [Economist Intelligence Unit](15303116.md)'s biannual Worldwide Cost of Living survey ranked Moscow 102nd among the 133 most expensive cities.[^cite_note-216] [ECA International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ECA_International)'s Cost of Living 2019 Survey ranked Moscow 120th among 482 locations globally.[^cite_note-217]

### Public utilities

Buildings in Moscow, as in other Russian cities, are warmed by [district heating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_heating). Before 2004, state [unitary enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_enterprise) were responsible for producing and supplying heat to clients by operating the heating stations and distribution system of Mosgorteplo, Mosteploenergo, and Teploremontnaladka; these provided service to the [heating substations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_heating_substation) in the [northeastern part](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Moscow) of the city. Clients were divided among enterprises according to location. A major reform launched in 2004 consolidated companies under MIPC, which became the municipal heat supplier. Its subsidiaries were new joint-stock companies. The city's main source of heating is Mosenergo's power station, which was reformed in 2005, when about ten subsidiaries were detached. One of the newly independent companies was the District Heating Network Company (MTK) ([Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language): Московская теплосетевая компания). In 2007, the [Government of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Moscow) bought controlling stakes in this company.[^cite_note-218]

"Our city" is a geoinformation portal created in 2011 under Moscow's mayor, Sergei Sobyanin, to initiate constructive dialogue between Moscow residents and the city's executive authorities. This portal is being developed by the State Public Institution "New Management Technologies", together with the Moscow Department of Information Technologies. During its 10 years of operation, more than 1.7 million users have registered for the portal; it has become effective for monitoring urban infrastructure.[^cite_note-219]

[Moscow State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_State_University)

Moscow contains 1,696 high schools and 91 colleges.[^cite_note-mosru-stats-155] In addition, the city has 222 institutions of higher education, including 60 state universities[^cite_note-mosru-stats-155] and the [Lomonosov Moscow State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_State_University) (which was founded in 1755).[^cite_note-msu-history-220] The main university building in Vorobyovy Gory (Sparrow Hills) is 240 meters (790 ft) tall; when completed, it was the tallest building on the continent.[^cite_note-221] The university enrols more than 30,000 undergraduate and 7,000 postgraduate students, who can choose among 29 faculties and 450 departments. The Moscow State University library contains more than nine million books, so it is one of the largest libraries in Russia.

The [I.M. Sechenov First Moscow State Medical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I.M._Sechenov_First_Moscow_State_Medical_University), named after [Ivan Sechenov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Sechenov), formerly known as [Moscow Medical Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Medical_Academy) (1stMSMU), is a medical university in Moscow. It was founded in 1785 as the faculty of Moscow State University. It is a Russian Federal Agency for Health and Social Development. Moreover, it is one of the largest medical universities in Russia and Europe. More than 9200 students are enrolled in 115 academic departments; the university also offers courses for postgraduate studies.



[Pirogov Russian National Research Medical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pirogov_Russian_National_Research_Medical_University)

The [Pirogov Russian National Research Medical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pirogov_Russian_National_Research_Medical_University) (formerly known as the [Russian State Medical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_State_Medical_University)) is a medical institution of higher education in Moscow, founded in 1906. This university is fully accredited and recognized by Russia's Ministry of Education and Science, and it is under the authority of the Ministry of Health and Social Development. Named after Russian surgeon and pedagogue [N.I. Pirogov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolay_Pirogov) (1810–1888), the university is one of the largest medical institutions in Russia, and the first in the country allowing women to complete degrees.

Moscow is one of the financial centers of the [Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Federation) and the [Commonwealth of Independent States](36870.md) (CIS), and the city is known for business schools. Among these are the [Financial University under the Government of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_University_under_the_Government_of_the_Russian_Federation), the [Plekhanov Russian University of Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plekhanov_Russian_University_of_Economics), the [State University of Management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_State_University_of_Management), and the [National Research University—Higher School of Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Research_University_-_Higher_School_of_Economics). These schools offer undergraduate degrees in management, finance, accounting, marketing, real estate, and economic theory; in addition, the schools offer master's degrees (including [Masters of Business Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Master_of_Business_Administration) \[MBAs]). Most of these schools have branches in other regions of Russia and in other countries.



The main building of the [Bauman Moscow State Technical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauman_Moscow_State_Technical_University)

[Bauman Moscow State Technical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauman_Moscow_State_Technical_University), founded in 1830, is located in the city center; the university educates 18,000 undergraduate and 1,000 postgraduate students in science and engineering, offering technical degrees.[^cite_note-222]



The [Moscow Conservatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Conservatory) building

The [Moscow Conservatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Conservatory),[^cite_note-223] founded in 1866, is a prominent music school in Russia.



The [Russian State Institute of Cinematography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerasimov_Institute_of_Cinematography), the world's oldest\
[film school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film_school)

The [Gerasimov All-Russian State Institute of Cinematography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerasimov_Institute_of_Cinematography) (VGIK) is the world's oldest educational institution in cinematography, founded by [Vladimir Gardin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Gardin) in 1919.

The [Moscow State Institute of International Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_State_Institute_of_International_Relations), founded in 1944, remains Russia's best-known school of international relations and diplomacy, with six schools focused on international relations. Approximately 4,500 students form the university's student body, and more than 700,000 Russian and foreign-language books (of which 20,000 are considered rare) are contained in the institute's library.[^cite_note-224]

Other Moscow institutions are the following:

- The [Moscow Institute of Physics and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Institute_of_Physics_and_Technology), also known as [Phystech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phystech), has taught numerous [Nobel Prize](21201.md) winners, including [Pyotr Kapitsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyotr_Kapitsa), [Nikolay Semyonov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolay_Semyonov), [Lev Landau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lev_Landau), and [Alexander Prokhorov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Prokhorov)

- the [Fyodorov Eye Microsurgery Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fyodorov_Eye_Microsurgery_Complex), founded in 1988 by Russian eye surgeon [Svyatoslav Fyodorov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Svyatoslav_Fyodorov)

- the [Moscow Aviation Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Aviation_Institute)

- the Moscow Motorway Institute (State Technical University)

- the [Moscow Engineering Physics Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Engineering_Physics_Institute), known for its research in [nuclear physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_physics)

  [^cite_note-225]

- the [Combined Arms Academy of the Armed Forces of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_Arms_Academy_of_the_Armed_Forces_of_the_Russian_Federation), Russia's highest military school

Although Moscow has a number of well-known Soviet-era higher educational institutions—most of which are oriented towards engineering or fundamental sciences—in recent years, the city has a growing number of commercial and private institutions that offer classes in business and management. Many state institutions have expanded their educational scope and introduced new courses or departments. Institutions in Moscow, like the rest of post-Soviet Russia, have begun to offer new international certificates and postgraduate degrees, including the Master of Business Administration. In addition, [student exchange programs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_exchange_program) with many countries—especially in Europe—have become widespread in Moscow's universities. Finally, schools in Moscow offer seminars, lectures, and courses for corporate employees and businessmen.



[Russian Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Academy_of_Sciences)

Moscow is one of the largest centers for science in Russia. The headquarters of the [Russian Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Academy_of_Sciences) are located in the city, as well as research and applied science institutions. Moscow also contains the following institutions:

- the [Kurchatov Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurchatov_Institute), Russia's leading research and development institution in nuclear energy, where the first [nuclear reactor](22151.md) in Europe was built

- the [Landau Institute for Theoretical Physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landau_Institute_for_Theoretical_Physics)

- the [Institute for Theoretical and Experimental Physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_Theoretical_and_Experimental_Physics)

- the [Kapitza Institute for Physical Problems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_Physical_Problems)

- the [Steklov Institute of Mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steklov_Institute_of_Mathematics)

The city contains 452 libraries, including 168 for children.[^cite_note-mosru-stats-155] The [Russian State Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_State_Library),[^cite_note-226] founded in 1862, is the national library of Russia. This library contains more than 275 kilometers (171 mi) of shelves and 42 million items—including more than 17 million books and serial volumes, 13 million journals, 350,000 music scores and sound records, and 150,000 maps—making it the country's largest library and one of the world's largest. Items in 247 languages other than Russian account for 29% of the library's collection.[^cite_note-227][^cite_note-228]

The State Public Historical Library, founded in 1863, is the largest library specialising in [Russian history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_history). Its collection contains four million items in 112 languages, mostly on Russian and world history, [heraldry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heraldry), [numismatics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numismatics), and the [history of science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_science).[^cite_note-229]

On the subject of primary and secondary education, journalist [Clifford J. Levy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifford_J._Levy) of *[The New York Times](30680.md)* newspaper wrote in 2011:[^cite_note-Levy1-230]

> Moscow has some strong public schools, but the system as a whole is dispiriting, in part because it is being corroded by the corruption that is a post-Soviet scourge. Parents often pay bribes to get their children admitted to better public schools. There are additional payoffs for good grades.

## Transportation

Main article: [Transportation in Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_Moscow)

### Metro

Main article: [Moscow Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Metro)



[Moscow Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Metro) route map with planned stations



[Mayakovskaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayakovskaya_\(Moscow_Metro\)) station, opened in 1938

The Moscow Metro system is well-known for art, [murals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mural), [mosaics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosaic), and [chandeliers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandelier). This system started operating in 1935 and promptly became the centerpiece of the city's transportation system. Moreover, the system was a Stalinist tactic to awe and reward the population, as well as help them to appreciate Soviet realist art. The system became the prototype for later large-scale Soviet technologies. [Lazar Kaganovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lazar_Kaganovich) headed the system; he designed the subway so that residents would absorb the values and ethos of Stalinist society as they rode. The artwork of the original 13 stations became nationally and internationally known. For example, the Sverdlov Square subway station featured porcelain bas-reliefs depicting the daily life of Soviet people; in addition, the bas-reliefs at the Dynamo Stadium sports complex glorified sports and the physical prowess of the new "Homo Sovieticus" (Soviet man).[^cite_note-231]

The metro was heralded as the symbol of a new social order—a sort of communist cathedral of engineering modernity.[^cite_note-232] Soviet workers performed the labor and created the art, but the main engineering designs, routes, and construction plans were handled by specialists recruited from the London Underground. The Britons called for tunneling rather than the "[cut-and-cover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cut-and-cover)" technique; preferred escalators over lifts; and designed the routes and the rolling stock.[^cite_note-233] The paranoia of Stalin and the [NKVD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NKVD) (Soviet secret police) was evident when the NKVD arrested many British engineers for espionage—that is, for gaining an in-depth knowledge of the city's physical layout. Engineers for the Metropolitan Vickers Electrical Company were given a show trial and deported in 1933, ending the role of British business in the USSR.[^cite_note-234]

Today, the Moscow Metro comprises 12 lines, mostly underground, with a total of 203 stations. It is one of the deepest subway systems in the world; for instance, the [Park Pobedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Pobedy_\(Moscow_Metro\)) station, completed in 2003, 84 meters (276 ft) underground, has the longest escalators in Europe. The Moscow Metro is the busiest such system in Europe, as well as [one of the world's busiest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_systems_by_annual_passenger_rides), serving about ten million passengers daily (300 million people each month).[^cite_note-metro-235] As of 2016, facing serious transportation problems, Moscow planned to expand its metro. That year, the authorities launched a [new circle metro railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Central_Circle) that helped to solve transportation problems, namely daily congestion on the Koltsevaya line.[^cite_note-236]

Because metro stations were treated as potential media for art—because workers in Moscow could see such art every day—many Stalin-era metro stations were built using customized designs; each station's design was initially a large installation on a specific theme. For example, the [Elektrozavodskaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elektrozavodskaya_\(Arbatsko%E2%80%93Pokrovskaya_line\)) station was themed on a nearby lightbulb factory and ceramic ribbed lightbulb sockets.[^cite_note-237] The tradition of "Grand Designs"—essentially decorating a metro station as a single-themed installation—was restored in late 1979.

Moscow's metro is one of the world's busiest, handling 2.6 billion passengers in 2019.[^cite_note-238]

Moscow contains more than 21,500 Wi-Fi access points in student dormitories; in parks; at cultural and sports institutions; and within the Garden Ring and the Third Transport Ring. From September 2020 to August 2021, 1,700 public Wi-Fi access points were launched in Moscow.[^cite_note-239] The structure of the city's Wi-Fi network allows residents to use the Internet without reauthorization.[^cite_note-240]

### Monorail

Main article: [Moscow Monorail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Monorail)



Two trains of the [Moscow Monorail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Monorail)

The Moscow Metro operates a short [monorail line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Monorail) (line 13). This line connects the [Timiryazevskaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timiryazevskaya_\(Serpukhovsko-Timiryazevskaya_Line\)) metro station and [Ulitsa Sergeya Eisensteina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulitsa_Sergeya_Eisensteina) (street), passing near the Exhibition of Achievements of National Economy (VDNKh) (and the Line 6 Metro station VDNKh). The monorail line opened in 2004. It permits above-ground transfers; a passenger does not need to pay any additional fare if they took another ride on the Moscow Metro within the most recent 90 minutes.

### Bus, trolleybus, and electric bus

Main article: [Electric buses in Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_buses_in_Moscow)



Moscow has the [largest fleet of electric buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_buses_in_Moscow) in Europe, with 500 operating as of October 2020.

[^cite_note-Trolleybus-241]

Because metro stations outside Moscow's city center are spaced relatively far apart from each other—up to 4 kilometers (2.5 mi)—compared to other cities, a radial bus network connects each station to the surrounding residential zones. Moscow has a bus terminal (the [Central Bus Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Central_Bus_Terminal)) for long-range and intercity passenger buses, handling about 25 thousand passengers each day and serving about 40% of long-range bus routes in Moscow.[^cite_note-242]

Every major street in Moscow is served by at least one bus route. Many of these routes share a trolleybus route and have overhead trolley wires.

The Moscow trolleybus system has a total line length of almost 600 kilometers (370 mi) of single wires, 8 depots, 104 routes, and 1740 vehicles; this system was the [world's largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trolleybus_usage_by_country). However, the municipal authority, headed by Sergey Sobyanin, began to phase out the trolleybus system in 2014 because of its planned replacement with electric buses. In 2018, the trolleybus system retained only 4 depots and dozens of kilometers of unused overhead wires. Almost all wires inside the Garden Ring (Sadovoe Koltso) were cut in 2016–2017 because the central streets were rebuilt (the "Moya Ulitsa" project). Opened on 15 November 1933, Moscow's trolleybus system is the world's sixth-oldest such system in operation.

In 2018, the vehicle companies [Kamaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamaz) and [GAZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GAZ) won the [Mosgortrans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosgortrans) contract to deliver 200 [electric buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_bus) and 62 ultrafast charging stations for the city's transport system. The manufacturers are responsible for the quality and reliable operation of buses and charging stations for 15 years. The city was procuring only electric buses as of 2021, gradually replacing the diesel bus fleet. Moscow was expected to become the leading European city in its share of electric versus gas fuel in public transit by 2019.[^cite_note-243]

### Moscow cable car

Main article: [Moskva River cable car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moskva_River_cable_car)



Cable cars passing over the [Moskva River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moskva_River) and the [Luzhniki Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzhniki_Stadium)

On 26 November 2018, the mayor of Moscow, Sergey Sobyanin, participated in the ceremony to launch the [cable car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moskva_River_cable_car) above the Moskva River. This car connects the [Luzhniki sports complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzhniki_Olympic_Complex) with Sparrow Hills and Kosygin Street. The trip from the viewpoint on Vorobyovy Gory to [Luzhniki Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzhniki_Stadium) takes only five minutes, rather than 20 minutes by car.

### Tram

Main article: [Trams in Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trams_in_Moscow)



A Vityaz-M tram passing by the [Tverskaya Zastava Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tverskaya_Zastava_Square)

Moscow has an extensive tram system, which opened in 1899.[^cite_note-244] The newest line was constructed in 1984. Daily tram usage by Muscovites is low, making up approximately 5% of trips, because vital connections in the network have been phased out. Nevertheless, trams remain important in some districts as feeders to metro stations. Trams also provide important cross-links between metro lines—for example, between the [Universitet station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universitet_\(Moscow_Metro\)) on the [Sokolnicheskaya Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sokolnicheskaya_Line) (#1 red line) and the [Profsoyuznaya station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Profsoyuznaya_\(Moscow_Metro\)) on the [Kaluzhsko-Rizhskaya Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaluzhsko-Rizhskaya_Line) (#6 orange line), and similarly between the [Voykovskaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voykovskaya_\(Moscow_Metro\)) metro station and the [Strogino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strogino_\(Moscow_Metro\)) metro station.



Tram map of Moscow

There are three tram networks in the city:

- The Krasnopresnenskoye depot network, with its westernmost point at Strogino (depot location) and its easternmost point near platform Dmitrovskaya. This network was divided in 1973; however, until 1997 it could have been easily reconnected using about 1 kilometer (50 chains) of track and three switches. The network has the highest usage in Moscow and no weak points based on traffic, except the to-depot lane (passengers serviced by bus) and the tram ring at Dmitrovskaya (because this is neither a normal transfer point nor a repair terminal).

- The Apakov depot services the southwestern portion, from Varshavsky Lane–Simferopolsky Boulevard in the east, to Universitet station in the west and Boulevard Lane at the center. This network is connected only by the four-way Dubininskaya and Kozhevnicheskaya Streets. A second connection by Vostochnaya (Eastern) Street was phased out in 1987 because of a fire at the Dinamo plant; this connection has not been recovered, and it remains lost (Avtozavodsky Bridge) as of 1992. In any case, the network may be serviced by another depot (routes 35 and 38).

- The main three depot networks with railway gate and tram-repair plant.

In addition, tram advocates have suggested that the new rapid transit services (metro to the city, Butovo light metro, monorail) would be more effective as at-grade tram lines and that the problems with trams are only due to poor management and operation, not the technical properties of trams. New tram models have been developed for the Moscow network despite the lack of expansion.

### Taxi

Commercial taxi services and [route taxis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Route_taxi) are widely used in Moscow. During the mid-2010s, service platforms such as [Yandex Taxi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yandex.Taxi), [Uber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uber), and [Gett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gett) displaced many private drivers and small service providers; in 2015, these platforms serviced more than 50% of all taxi orders in the city.[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246]

As of 2021, Russian technology company [Yandex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yandex) was testing self-driving taxis in Moscow.[^cite_note-247]

### Railway



[Komsomolskaya Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Komsomolskaya_Square_\(Moscow\)) is known as "Three Station Square" because of its three ornate rail terminals: [Leningradsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Passazhirskaya_railway_station), [Yaroslavsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Yaroslavskaya_railway_station), and [Kazansky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Kazanskaya_railway_station).

&#x20;Several train stations serve Moscow. The city's ten rail terminals (or *vokzals*) are the following:

- [Belorussky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belorussky_Rail_Terminal)

- [Kazansky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazansky_Rail_Terminal)

- [Kiyevsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiyevsky_Rail_Terminal)

- [Kursky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kursky_Rail_Terminal)

- [Leningradsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leningradsky_Rail_Terminal)

- [Paveletsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paveletsky_Rail_Terminal)

- [Rizhsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizhsky_Rail_Terminal)

- [Savyolovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savyolovsky_Rail_Terminal)

- [Yaroslavsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaroslavsky_Rail_Terminal)

- [Vostochny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vostochny_Railway_Terminal_\(Moscow\))



The high-speed [Sapsan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sapsan) train links Moscow with [Saint Petersburg](24320051.md).

These terminals are near the city center—along the Metro's Line 5 or near it—and connect to it via a metro line. Each station handles trains from different parts of Europe and Asia.[^cite_note-mostimestrain-248] Moscow contains many smaller railway stations. Because tickets are inexpensive, trains are Russians' preferred mode of travel, especially when traveling to Saint Petersburg. Moscow is the western terminus of the [Trans-Siberian Railway](56912.md), which crosses nearly 9,300 kilometers (5,800 mi) of Russian territory to reach [Vladivostok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladivostok) on the [Pacific](23070.md) coast.

Suburbs and satellite cities are connected by a commuter [*elektrichka*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elektrichka) (electric rail) network. *Elektrichkas* depart from each of these terminals to the nearby large railway stations (up to 140 kilometers or 87 miles away).

During the 2010s, the [Little Ring of the Moscow Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Ring_of_the_Moscow_Railway) was converted for frequent passenger service. This ring is fully integrated with the Moscow Metro; passenger service started on 10 September 2016. A connecting railway line on the city's north side connects the Belorussky terminal with other railway lines. This line is used by some suburban trains.

#### Moscow Central Circle



"Lastochka" train at the [Luzhniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzhniki_\(Moscow_Central_Circle\)) station (Line 14)

The [Moskovskaya Okruzhnaya Zheleznaya Doroga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Ring_of_the_Moscow_Railway) (orbital railway line) has formed a ring around Moscow's current downtown since 1903. However, this line was a non-electrified, locomotive-only railway before reconstruction into the MCC during the 2010s.

The [Moscow Central Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Central_Circle) is a 54-kilometer (34 mi) orbital [urban rail transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_rail_transit) line that encircles historic Moscow. This line was built alongside the [Little Ring of the Moscow Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Ring_of_the_Moscow_Railway), incorporating some of its tracks. The MCC was opened for passenger use on 10 September 2016.

The MCC line is operated by the [Moscow government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Government)-owned company MKZD through the Moscow Metro, with the federal government-owned [Russian Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Railways) as the operations subcontractor.

#### Moscow Central Diameters



An [EG2Tv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivolga_\(train\)) train arriving at the [Moscow Belorussky railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Belorussky_railway_station)



Map of the [Moscow Central Diameters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Central_Diameters)

The [Moscow Central Diameters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Central_Diameters) are another system, forming a "genuine *S-Bahn*" as a railway designed for "suburbia-city-suburbia" use. They are a pass-through railway system, created by constructing bypasses from "vokzals" (terminal stations)—for example, by avoiding the central stations of the pre-existing Moscow Railway, previously used for both intercity and urban-suburban travel[^cite_note-249]—and forming a train line across Moscow's center.

Of the five lines projected for the Moscow Central Diameters, the first two were completed and launched on 21 November 2019.

### Roads



Intersection at [Tverskaya Zastava Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tverskaya_Zastava_Square)

There are more than 2.6 million cars in Moscow each day. This number has grown in recent years; as a result, traffic jams and insufficient parking space have become major problems.

The Moscow Ring Road (MKAD)—along with the [Third Transport Ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Ring_Road_\(Moscow\)) and the canceled Fourth Transport Ring—is one of only three freeways running within city limits. Several other roadway systems form [concentric circles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concentric_circles) around the city.

### Air

Five primary commercial airports serve Moscow: [Sheremetyevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheremetyevo_International_Airport) (SVO), [Domodedovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domodedovo_International_Airport) (DME), [Vnukovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vnukovo_International_Airport) (VKO), [Zhukovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhukovsky_International_Airport) (ZIA), [Ostafyevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostafyevo_International_Airport) (OSF).



Sheremetyevo, the [busiest airport in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_busiest_airports_in_Russia), is ranked as the [11th-busiest airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_busiest_airports_in_Europe) in Europe.

Sheremetyevo International Airport is Moscow's most globally connected airport, handling 60% of international flights.[^cite_note-4airports-250] This airport is a hub for all members of the [SkyTeam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SkyTeam) airline alliance, as well as the main hub for the airline [Aeroflot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeroflot) (a member of SkyTeam). Domodedovo International Airport is Russia's leading airport in passenger traffic, and is the primary gateway to long-haul domestic and CIS destinations; its international traffic rivals that of Sheremetyevo. Domodedovo is a hub for [S7 Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S7_airlines), and most [OneWorld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OneWorld) and [Star Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_Alliance) members use Domodedovo as their international hub. Vnukovo International Airport handles flights for [Turkish Airlines](167925.md), [Wizz Air Abu Dhabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wizz_Air_Abu_Dhabi), and other airlines. Ostafyevo International Airport handles primarily business aviation.

Moscow's airports vary in distance from the MKAD beltway. A list of these airports from farthest to nearest is as follows:

- Domodedovo—22 kilometers (14 mi)

- Vnukovo—11 kilometers (7 mi)

- Sheremetyevo—10 kilometers (6 mi)

- Ostafievo—about 8 kilometers (5.0 mi)

  [^cite_note-4airports-250]

A number of smaller airports are located near Moscow—19 in Moscow Oblast. An example is [Myachkovo Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myachkovo_Airport). These airports are intended for private aircraft, helicopters, and charters.[^cite_note-251]

### Water

Moscow has two passenger terminals ([South River Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_River_Terminal) and [North River Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_River_Terminal)) on the Moskva River. There are regular ship routes and cruises along the [Moskva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moskva_River) and [Oka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oka_River) rivers, which are used mostly for entertainment. The [North River Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_River_Terminal), built in 1937, is the main hub for long-range river routes. In addition, three freight ports serve Moscow.

Moscow is connected via the [Moscow Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Canal) to Russia's [Unified Deep Water System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unified_Deep_Water_System_of_European_Russia), a large system of canals and rivers in European Russia. This system gives the city water access to five seas: the [White Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Sea), Baltic Sea, [Caspian Sea](19653787.md), [Sea of Azov](78019.md), and the [Black Sea](3386.md). For this reason, Moscow is sometimes called the "port of the five seas" ([Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language): порт пяти морей).[^cite_note-252]

### Vehicle sharing system

See also: [Carsharing in Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carsharing_in_Moscow)



As of 2020, Moscow has the world's [largest fleet of carsharing vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carsharing_in_Moscow), with more than 30,000 cars.

[^cite_note-Carsharing-253]

Moscow offers vehicle sharing options that are sponsored by the local government. Several [car-sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Car_sharing) companies are responsible for providing cars to the population. To drive one of these cars, a user must book it through the app (application software) of the owning company. In 2018, Mayor Sergey Sobyanin said that Moscow's car-sharing system had become Europe's largest fleet.[^cite_note-254] Every day, about 25,000 people used this service. By the end of that year, Moscow carsharing had become the world's second largest, with 16,500 vehicles available.[^cite_note-255] Another sharing system is [bike sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bike_sharing) (Moscow's *[Velobike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Velobike) system*), with a fleet of 3000 traditional and electric bicycles.[^cite_note-256] The *Delisamokat* is a new sharing service providing electric scooters.[^cite_note-257]

### Future development



The 2020 development concept of the [Moscow International Business Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_International_Business_Center) and its adjacent territory implies the construction of more skyscrapers from 2020 to 2027.

[^cite_note-258]

[^cite_note-259]

In 1992, the Moscow government began planning a new area in central Moscow—the Moscow International Business Center—to create a zone, the first in Russia and Eastern Europe,[^cite_note-260] to combine business activity, living space, and entertainment. Situated in the [Presnensky District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presnensky_District) and located at the Third Ring, the Moscow City area is undergoing intensive development. The Moscow International Business Center (MIBC) is being constructed on the Krasnopresnenskaya embankment. The project occupies up to one square kilometer (250 acres). That area is the only location in downtown Moscow able to accommodate a project of such size. Most pre-existing buildings in that area are old factories and industrial complexes.

Demonstrating the MIBC's scale, the [Federation Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_Tower), completed in 2016, is the [second-tallest building in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_Europe). The project's broader plans include a [water park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_park) and other recreational facilities; business, office, entertainment, and residential buildings; a transport network; and a [new site for the Moscow government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Hall_and_City_Duma). Four new metro stations in the area have been built; two of these have opened, and two others are reserved for future metro lines crossing the MIBC. Additional stations were planned.

In addition, a rail shuttle service is planned to directly connect the MIBC with the [Sheremetyevo International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheremetyevo_International_Airport). Major thoroughfares through the MIBC are the [Third Ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Ring_Road_\(Moscow\)) and [Kutuzovsky Prospekt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutuzovsky_Prospekt).

Three metro stations were initially planned for the [Filyovskaya Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filyovskaya_Line). The Delovoi Tsentr station opened in 2005 and was renamed [Vystavochnaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vystavochnaya) in 2009. The branch extended to the [Mezhdunarodnaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mezhdunarodnaya_\(Moscow_Metro\)) station in 2006; all work on the third station, Dorogomilovskaya (between [Kiyevskaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiyevskaya_\(Filyovskaya\)) and Delovoi Tsentr), has been postponed. The branch is planned for extension as far as the [Savyolovskaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savyolovskaya_\(Serpukhovsko%E2%80%93Timiryazevskaya_line\)) station, on the [Serpukhovsko-Timiryazevskaya Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serpukhovsko-Timiryazevskaya_Line).

On 5 March 2021, the company [MTS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MTS_\(telecommunications\)) announced the launch of the country's first pilot [5G](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5G) network in Moscow.[^cite_note-261]

## Media

See also: [Media of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Russia)

Nearly all of Russia's nationwide [television networks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_Russia), [radio stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_station), newspapers, and magazines are headquartered in Moscow.

### Newspapers

Further information: [List of newspapers in Russia](10634715.md)

English-language newspapers include *[The Moscow Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Moscow_Times)*.[^cite_note-262] Until its closure in 2014, *[The Moscow News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Moscow_News)* was the oldest English-language weekly newspaper in Russia. *[Kommersant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kommersant)*, *[Vedomosti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedomosti)*, and *[Novaya Gazeta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novaya_Gazeta)* are Russian-language newspapers headquartered in Moscow. *Kommersant* and *Vedomosti* are among the country's oldest Russian-language business newspapers.

### Television and radio

See also: [Television in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_Russia)



The [RTRN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Television_and_Radio_Broadcasting_Network) building

Other media in Moscow include the *[Echo of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Echo_of_Moscow)* ([Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language): Эхо Москвы), the first Soviet and Russian private news radio and information agency, and [NTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NTV_\(Russia\)), one of the first privately owned Russian television stations. The number of FM radio stations in Moscow totals about 50.

## Notable people

Main article: [List of people from Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_from_Moscow)

Further information: [Category\:People from Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:People_from_Moscow)

- Writer Alexander Pushkin, the founder of modern Russian literature, was born in Moscow in 1799.

  Writer [Alexander Pushkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Pushkin), the founder of modern [Russian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_literature), was born in Moscow in 1799.

- Novelist and philosopher Fyodor Dostoevsky was born in Moscow in 1821.

  Novelist and philosopher [Fyodor Dostoevsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fyodor_Dostoevsky) was born in Moscow in 1821.

- Military commander Alexander Suvorov was born in Moscow in 1730.

  Military commander [Alexander Suvorov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Suvorov) was born in Moscow in 1730.

- Tsar Peter the Great was born in Moscow in 1672.

  Tsar [Peter the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_the_Great) was born in Moscow in 1672.
